# Glossary of logic

## o1 subject: Glossary of logic

This is a glossary of logic. Logic is the study of the principles of valid reasoning and argumentation.

## o2 section: A

## o3 entry: A-proposition

A-proposition
A type of standard-form categorical proposition, asserting that all members of the subject category are included in the predicate category; symbolized as "All S are P".

## o4 citation

## o5 citation: IEP_Square

## o6 entry: abduction

abduction
A form of reasoning characterized by drawing a conclusion based on the best available explanation for a set of premises. Often used in hypothesis formation.

## o7 entry: Abelian logic

Abelian logic
A type of relevance logic that rejects contraction and accepts that ((A → B) → B) → A.

## o8 citation

## o9 citation

## o10 citation

## o11 entry: absorption

absorption
A logical rule stating that if a proposition implies another, then adding any additional conjunction to the first proposition does not change the implication. Symbolized as $P \rightarrow Q \equiv (P \land R) \rightarrow Q$.

## o12 entry: abstract object

abstract object
An object that does not exist at any particular time or place but rather exists as a type of thing—i.e., an idea, or abstraction.

## o13 entry: abstraction

abstraction
The process or result of generalization by reducing the information content of a concept or an observable phenomenon, typically to retain only information which is relevant for a particular purpose.

## o14 entry: abstraction operator

abstraction operator
The function implicitly defined by an abstraction principle.

## o15 citation: CookDictionary

## o16 entry: abstraction principle

abstraction principle
A formula of the form (∀α)(∀β)(Abst(α) = Abst(β) ↔ Equ(α, β)), where Abst is an abstraction operator mapping the type of entities ranged over by α and β to objects, and “Equ” is an equivalence relation on the type of entities ranged over by α and β. For instance, Hume's principle, and Basic Law V.

## o17 entry: accessibility relation

accessibility relation
In modal logic, a relation that describes which worlds are accessible from other worlds in the semantics of possible world models.

## o18 entry: actual world

actual world
In modal logic, the world that is considered to be the real world, as opposed to possible worlds which are considered in modal reasoning.

## o19 entry: addition

addition
A rule of inference in formal logic where from any proposition, a disjunction can be formed by disjoining it with any other proposition. Symbolized as $P \vdash P \lor Q$.

## o20 entry: ad hominem

ad hominem
A fallacy in argumentation that targets the person making an argument rather than the argument itself.

## o21 entry: ad ignorantium

ad ignorantium
A logical fallacy where a proposition is considered true because it has not been proven false or vice versa.

## o22 entry: ad infinitum

ad infinitum
An argument or process that is supposed to continue indefinitely, without ever reaching an end or conclusion.

## o23 entry: adjunction

adjunction
See conjunction introduction.

## o24 entry: affine logics

affine logics
A subfield of linear logic focusing on the study of affine transformations and their implications in logical reasoning.

## o25 entry: affirmative proposition

affirmative proposition
A proposition that asserts the truth of a statement, as opposed to negating it.

## o26 citation: comfsm

## o27 citation: elemen38

## o28 citation: lander-quantqual

## o29 entry: affirming the consequent

affirming the consequent
A logical fallacy in which a conditional statement is incorrectly used to infer its converse. For example, from "If P then Q" and "Q", concluding "P".

## o30 entry: alethic modal logic

alethic modal logic
A type of modal logic that deals with modalities of truth, such as necessity and possibility.

## o31 entry: ambiguity

ambiguity
The property of a statement or phrase having more than one meaning or interpretation.

## o32 entry: amphiboly

amphiboly
A type of ambiguity resulting from ambiguous grammatical structure, rather than the ambiguity of individual words.

## o33 entry: analethic logic

analethic logic
A three-valued logic where the third truth value is the truth-value gap "neither true nor false" ("N"), and the designated value are "true" and "neither true nor false."

## o34 citation: WrongWithTonk

## o35 entry: analysis

analysis
Analysis, the process of breaking a concept down into more simple parts, so that its logical structure is displayed.
Mathematical analysis

## o36 entry: analytic

analytic
A statement that is true by virtue of its meaning, such as definitions or statements grounded in logical laws.

## o37 entry: anaphora

anaphora
A rhetorical device that consists of repeating a sequence of words at the beginnings of neighboring clauses, thereby lending them emphasis.

## o38 entry: ancestral

ancestral
In logic and mathematics, the transitive closure of a relation, capturing the idea of indirect relationships across generations or iterations.

## o39 entry: antecedent

antecedent
The first part of a conditional statement, the "if" clause, which specifies a condition for the consequent.

## o40 entry: anti-extension

anti-extension
In set theory and logic, the complement of the extension of a concept or predicate, consisting of all objects that do not fall under the concept.

## o41 citation

## o42 citation

## o43 citation

## o44 entry: antilogism

antilogism
A syllogism with three premises leading to a contradiction, showing the inconsistency of the premises.

## o45 citation

## o46 citation

## o47 citation

## o48 entry: antinomy

antinomy
A contradiction between two beliefs or conclusions that are in themselves reasonable; a paradox.

## o49 entry: antisymmetry

antisymmetry
A property of a relation $R$, where for any two elements $a$ and $b$, if $aRb$ and $bRa$, then $a = b$.

## o50 entry: a posteriori

a posteriori
Knowledge or justification is said to be a posteriori if it depends on empirical evidence or experience.

## o51 entry: a priori

a priori
Knowledge or justification is said to be a priori if it is independent of empirical evidence or experience, such as logic or mathematics.

## o52 entry: argument

argument
A series of statements typically used to persuade someone of something or to present reasons for accepting a conclusion.

## o53 entry: Aristotelian comprehension schema

Aristotelian comprehension schema
This formula in second-order logic: (∃x)Φ → (∃Y)(∀x)(Yx ↔ Φ).

## o54 entry: Aristotelian logic

Aristotelian logic
The traditional logic developed by Aristotle, based on the study of syllogism and the principle of non-contradiction.

## o55 entry: Aristotle's sea battle

Aristotle's sea battle
A thought experiment by Aristotle to explore the concept of future contingents and the problem of determinism and free will.

## o56 entry: Aristotle's theses

Aristotle's theses
The formulas ¬ (¬ A → A) and ¬ (A → ¬A) in propositional logic; they are theorems in connexive logic but not in classical logic. See also Boethius' theses.

## o57 citation

## o58 citation

## o59 citation

## o60 entry: arity

arity
The number of arguments or operands that a function, operation, or relation takes. In logic, it refers to the number of terms that a predicate has.

## o61 entry: assertion

assertion
The principle, or axiom, that (A ∧ (A → B)) → B. Also called pseudo modus ponens.

## o62 citation

## o63 citation

## o64 entry: associativity

associativity
A property of some binary operations in which the grouping of operations does not affect the result. For example, in arithmetic, addition and multiplication are associative.

## o65 entry: asymmetry

asymmetry
A relation between two entities where if the first is related to the second, the second is not related to the first.

## o66 entry: atomic formula

atomic formula
The simplest type of formula in logic, consisting of a single predicate applied to a sequence of terms without any logical connectives.

## o67 entry: atomic sentence

atomic sentence
A sentence that contains no logical connectives or quantifiers, expressing a basic statement about objects.

## o68 entry: autological

autological
A term that describes itself. For example, the word "short" is autological because it is a short word.

## o69 entry: automaton

automaton
A self-operating machine or, in computer science, a theoretical model of computation that performs tasks according to a set of rules or a program.

## o70 entry: automorphism

automorphism
An isomorphism from a mathematical object to itself, preserving all the structure of the object. In logic, it often refers to symmetries within logical structures.

## o71 entry: axiological logic

axiological logic
A branch of logic that deals with the study of value, including ethical and aesthetic values, often in the context of modal logic.

## o72 citation

## o73 citation

## o74 citation

## o75 entry: axiom

axiom
A statement or proposition that is accepted as true without proof, serving as a starting point for further reasoning and arguments.

## o76 section: B

## o77 entry: bad company objection

bad company objection
An objection to mathematical abstractionism that points out the difficulty in distinguishing between legitimate and illegitimate forms of abstraction, particularly in the context of Frege's Basic Law V and the paradoxes it generates.

## o78 citation

## o79 citation

## o80 citation

## o81 entry: barbara

barbara
A standard form of categorical syllogism in Aristotelian logic, where all three propositions (major premise, minor premise, and conclusion) are universal affirmatives, symbolized as AAA. The form is: All M are P, All S are M, therefore All S are P.

## o82 citation

## o83 citation

## o84 citation

## o85 entry: Barcan formula

Barcan formula
A principle in modal logic that asserts the interchangeability of quantification and possibility: necessarily, if there exists something, then there necessarily exists something.

## o86 entry: Basic Law V

Basic Law V
A principle proposed by Gottlob Frege in his attempt to reduce arithmetic to logic, stating that the extension of a concept is determined by the objects falling under the concept. It leads to Russell's paradox.

## o87 entry: basis

basis
The initial case in a proof by mathematical induction.

## o88 entry: Bayes' theorem

Bayes' theorem
A theorem in probability theory used to update the probability for a hypothesis as more evidence or information becomes available.

## o89 entry: begging the question

begging the question
A logical fallacy where the conclusion of an argument is assumed in the premise, making the argument circular.

## o90 entry: Bew

Bew
See provability predicate.

## o91 entry: BHK-interpretation

BHK-interpretation
The Brouwer-Heyting-Kolmogorov interpretation, a constructivist interpretation of intuitionistic logic, where the truth of a statement is equated with the existence of a proof for it.

## o92 entry: bias

bias
A systematic deviation from neutrality, objectivity, or fairness, often resulting from a particular tendency or inclination, especially in statistical or cognitive contexts.

## o93 entry: biconditional

biconditional
A logical connective between statements, where both statements imply each other; often denoted as $P \leftrightarrow Q$, meaning "P if and only if Q".

## o94 entry: bijective

bijective
A function that is both injective (no two elements of the domain map to the same element of the codomain) and surjective (every element of the codomain is mapped to by some element of the domain), establishing a one-to-one correspondence between the domain and codomain.

## o95 entry: binary function

binary function
A function that takes two arguments. In logic and mathematics, this is often a function that combines two values to produce a third value, such as addition or multiplication in arithmetic.

## o96 entry: binary relation

binary relation
A relation involving two terms or elements, defining a particular relationship between pairs of objects from two sets (or from one set to itself).

## o97 entry: bivalence

bivalence
The principle that any proposition is either true or false, with no middle ground; foundational to classical logic.

## o98 entry: Boethius' theses

Boethius' theses
The formulas (A → B) → ¬ (A → ¬ B) and (A → ¬ B) → ¬ (A → B) in propositional logic; they are theorems in connexive logic but not in classical logic. See also Aristotle's theses.

## o99 citation

## o100 citation

## o101 citation

## o102 entry: Boolean algebra

Boolean algebra
An area of algebra in which the values of the variables are the truth values true and false, typically used in computer science, logic, and mathematical logic.

## o103 entry: Boolean negation

Boolean negation
A form of negation where the negation of a non-true proposition is true, and the negation of a non-false proposition is false.

## o104 citation

## o105 citation

## o106 citation

## o107 entry: Boolean operator

Boolean operator
An operator used in logic and computer science that performs logical operations on its operands, such as AND, OR, and NOT.

## o108 entry: borderline case

borderline case
A situation or instance that falls at the boundary between categories or classifications, often challenging strict definitions or distinctions.

## o109 citation: SEP_Vagueness

## o110 citation

## o111 citation

## o112 citation

## o113 entry: bottom

bottom
In logic, the symbol used to represent a contradiction or an absurdity; often denoted by the symbol $\bot$.

## o114 entry: bound variable

bound variable
A variable that is quantified over in a logical expression, as opposed to a free variable, which is not bound by a quantifier.

## o115 entry: bounded quantifier

bounded quantifier
A quantifier that operates within a specific domain or set, as opposed to an unbounded or universal quantifier that applies to all elements of a particular type.

## o116 entry: branching quantifier

branching quantifier
A type of quantifier in formal logic that allows for the expression of dependencies between different quantified variables, representing more complex relationships than can be expressed with standard linear quantification.

## o117 entry: Brouwerian modal logic

Brouwerian modal logic
A form of modal logic that incorporates principles of intuitionism, as developed by L.E.J. Brouwer, focusing on the notion of possibility grounded in constructivist or intuitionist mathematics.

## o118 citation

## o119 citation

## o120 citation

## o121 entry: Buridan's sophismata

Buridan's sophismata
A collection of paradoxes and logical exercises attributed to the medieval philosopher Jean Buridan, designed to challenge logical and linguistic intuitions.

## o122 citation

## o123 citation

## o124 citation

## o125 entry: busy beaver problem

busy beaver problem
A problem in computability theory that seeks the Turing machine with the largest possible behavior (e.g., producing the most output, running the longest) among all Turing machines of a certain size, illustrating limits of computability.

## o126 section: C

## o127 entry: Caesar problem

Caesar problem
A problem in the philosophy of language and logic regarding the applicability of mathematical concepts to non-mathematical objects, famously illustrated by Gottlob Frege's question of whether the concept of being a successor in number applies to Julius Caesar.

## o128 citation

## o129 citation

## o130 citation

## o131 entry: Carnap-Ramsey sentence

Carnap-Ramsey sentence
A sentence formulated to express the empirical content of a theory in logical positivism, named after Rudolf Carnap and Frank P. Ramsey, aimed at separating theoretical terms from observational terms.

## o132 entry: categorical

categorical
A theory is categorical if all its models are isomorphic, meaning they describe the same mathematical structures in different ways.

## o133 entry: categorical logic

categorical logic
A branch of logic that studies the categorization of objects and the logical foundations of categories, often using the framework of category theory.

## o134 entry: categorical proposition

categorical proposition
A proposition that asserts or denies that all or some of the members of one category are included in another category, fundamental in syllogistic reasoning.

## o135 entry: categorical syllogism

categorical syllogism
A form of deductive reasoning in Aristotelian logic consisting of three categorical propositions that involve three terms and deduce a conclusion from two premises.

## o136 entry: category

category
In mathematics and logic, a collection of objects and morphisms between them that satisfies certain axioms, fundamental to category theory.

## o137 entry: category theory

category theory
A branch of mathematics that deals with abstract algebraic structures and relationships between them, providing a unifying framework for various areas of mathematics.

## o138 entry: causal logic

causal logic
A branch of logic concerned with the study of causal relationships, including the representation and reasoning about causes and effects.

## o139 citation

## o140 citation

## o141 entry: causal modal logic

causal modal logic
An extension of modal logic that includes modalities for necessity and possibility along with causal relations, allowing for the formal analysis of causal statements.

## o142 citation

## o143 citation

## o144 entry: chronological logic

chronological logic
See temporal modal logic.

## o145 citation

## o146 citation

## o147 entry: Church's theorem

Church's theorem
A theorem establishing the undecidability of certain decision problems in logic, such as the Entscheidungsproblem, proving that there is no consistent, complete, and effectively calculable logic.

## o148 entry: Church–Turing thesis

Church–Turing thesis
A hypothesis proposing that any function that can be naturally regarded as computable by a human being can be computed by a Turing machine, thereby defining the limits of what can be computed.

## o149 entry: classical dilemma

classical dilemma
A form of argument presenting two alternatives, both leading to the same conclusion, often used in classical rhetoric and logic to demonstrate inevitability.

## o150 entry: classical logic

classical logic
The traditional framework of logic based on principles of bivalence, non-contradiction, and excluded middle, primarily focusing on propositional and predicate logic.

## o151 entry: classical reductio ad absurdum

classical reductio ad absurdum
A stronger form of reductio ad absurdum, where instead of only deriving $\neg P$ from showing that $P$ leads to a contradiction, one can also derive $P$ from showing that $\neg P$ leads to a contradiction.

## o152 citation

## o153 entry: coextensive

coextensive
Having the same scope or range, especially referring to two terms or concepts that apply to the same set of objects.

## o154 citation

## o155 citation

## o156 entry: cogent inductive argument

cogent inductive argument
A strong inductive argument where all the premises are true.

## o157 citation

## o158 citation

## o159 citation

## o160 citation

## o161 entry: coherence theory of truth

coherence theory of truth
A theory that holds truth as coherence among a set of beliefs or statements, rather than correspondence with reality or facts.

## o162 entry: combinator

combinator
A function or expression in combinatory logic that acts on arguments to produce results without the need for variable bindings.

## o163 entry: combinatorialism

combinatorialism
Combinatorialism is the view that any arbitrary combination of elements constitutes a legitimate mathematical structure, whether that structure is definable or not.

## o164 citation

## o165 citation

## o166 citation

## o167 entry: combinatory logic

combinatory logic
A branch of mathematical logic that seeks to eliminate the need for variables in mathematical expressions by using combinators.

## o168 entry: combinatory terms

combinatory terms
See combinator.

## o169 entry: commutativity

commutativity
A property of binary operations where the order of the operands does not change the result, as in addition and multiplication in arithmetic.

## o170 entry: compactness theorem

compactness theorem
A theorem in logic stating that if every finite subset of a set of sentences has a model, then the entire set has a model.

## o171 entry: complete infinity

complete infinity
A concept in philosophy and mathematics referring to an actual infinity that is considered as a completed whole, contrasting with potential infinities that are indefinitely extendable.

## o172 entry: completeness

completeness
In logic, the property of a logical system whereby every logically valid formula can be derived using the system's axioms and inference rules.

## o173 entry: complexity class

complexity class
A classification of decision problems based on their inherent computational complexity, grouping problems that can be solved within similar resource constraints.

## o174 entry: complexity theory

complexity theory
The study of the inherent difficulty of computational problems, focusing on the resources needed to solve them, such as time and memory.

## o175 entry: composition

composition
The act of combining simple objects or ideas into a complex whole, or the resulting combination itself.

## o176 entry: compositionality

compositionality
The principle in semantics that the meaning of a complex expression is determined by the meanings of its constituent expressions and the rules used to combine them.

## o177 entry: compound formula

compound formula
A formula representing a compound statement.

## o178 citation

## o179 entry: compound statement

compound statement
A statement in logic that is formed by combining two or more statements with logical connectives, allowing for the construction of more complex statements from simpler ones.

## o180 citation: IntroLogic2

## o181 citation: IEP_PropositionalLogic

## o182 entry: comprehension schema

comprehension schema
A principle in set theory and logic allowing for the formation of sets based on a defining property or condition.

## o183 entry: computable function

computable function
A function for which there exists an algorithm that can compute the function's value for any valid input within a finite amount of time.

## o184 entry: computation

computation
The process of performing a sequence of calculations or logical operations systematically to obtain a result.

## o185 entry: concept

concept
An abstract idea representing the fundamental characteristics of what it describes.

## o186 entry: conclusion

conclusion
The statement that logically follows from the premises of an argument, serving as the argument's final claim.

## o187 entry: concrete object

concrete object
An object that exists in physical reality, as opposed to abstract objects which do not have a physical existence.

## o188 entry: conditional

conditional
A logical connective that represents a "if... then..." relationship between two statements.

## o189 entry: conditional logic

conditional logic
A branch of logic that studies the properties of the conditional connective and related logical concepts.

## o190 citation

## o191 citation

## o192 entry: conditional probability

conditional probability
The probability of an event occurring given that another event has already occurred.

## o193 entry: conditional proof

conditional proof
A method in logic for proving a conditional statement by assuming the antecedent and showing that the consequent follows.

## o194 entry: conditionalization

conditionalization
The conditional obtained by taking the conjunction of the premises of the argument as antecedent and the conclusion of the argument as consequent. For instance, the conditionalization of modus ponens, $P \to Q, P \vdash Q$, is the formula $((P \to Q) \land P) \to Q$, called pseudo modus ponens.

## o195 entry: congruence relation

congruence relation
An equivalence relation that respects the operations of the algebraic structure it is defined on, such as addition or multiplication in a group.

## o196 entry: conjunct

conjunct
A component statement within a conjunction, each of which must be true for the entire conjunction to be true.

## o197 citation: beallbasics1ed

## o198 entry: conjunction

conjunction
A logical connective that represents the "and" relationship between two statements, requiring both to be true.

## o199 entry: conjunction elimination

conjunction elimination
A rule of inference in propositional logic that allows one to infer a conjunct from a conjunction.

## o200 entry: conjunction introduction

conjunction introduction
A rule of inference that allows the formation of a conjunction from two individual statements.

## o201 entry: conjunctive normal form

conjunctive normal form
A way of expressing a logical formula as a conjunction of clauses, where each clause is a disjunction of literals.

## o202 entry: connected

connected
A property of a graph in which there is a path between any two vertices, or a property of a topological space in which it cannot be divided into two disjoint nonempty open sets.

## o203 entry: connexive logic

connexive logic
A branch of logic that studies principles of connection between propositions, such as the relation between a statement and its contrapositive.

## o204 entry: consequence relation

consequence relation
A relation between sets of sentences or propositions, where the truth of the first set (the premises) necessitates the truth of the second set (the consequences).

## o205 entry: consequent

consequent
The second part of a conditional statement, indicating the result or outcome if the antecedent condition is met.

## o206 entry: consequentia mirabilis

consequentia mirabilis
A principle in classical logic stating that if the negation of a proposition leads to a contradiction, then the proposition itself must be true.

## o207 entry: conservative extension

conservative extension
A property of a theory that is expanded by new axioms or rules without changing the truth values of statements in the original theory.

## o208 entry: constant

constant
A symbol in logic and mathematics that represents a fixed and unchanging value.

## o209 entry: constant function

constant function
A function that always returns the same value, regardless of the input.

## o210 entry: constructive dilemma

constructive dilemma
A form of argument where, given two conditional statements and evidence that at least one of their antecedents is true, one can conclude that at least one of the consequents is true.

## o211 entry: constructive logic

constructive logic
A branch of logic that emphasizes the constructive proof of existence, requiring an explicit construction of an object to assert its existence rather than relying on indirect arguments.

## o212 entry: constructive mathematics

constructive mathematics
A philosophy of mathematics that requires mathematical objects to be constructible and computable, rejecting non-constructive proofs such as those involving the law of excluded middle in its full generality.

## o213 entry: constructive proof

constructive proof
A proof that demonstrates the existence of a mathematical object by providing a method to construct it explicitly, as opposed to proving indirectly by contradiction.

## o214 entry: contextualism

contextualism
The theory that the context in which an assertion is made is essential for understanding its truth conditions and meaning.

## o215 entry: contingency

contingency
The property of being neither necessarily true nor necessarily false, depending on some conditions or on the state of the world.

## o216 entry: contraction

contraction
In logic and mathematics, the process of eliminating duplicates in expressions or equations to simplify them.

## o217 entry: contradiction

contradiction
A statement or proposition that asserts both a statement and its negation, considered universally false in classical logic.

## o218 entry: contradictory

contradictory
Referring to a pair of statements or propositions where one is the negation of the other, such that they cannot both be true or both be false.

## o219 entry: contraposition

contraposition
A logical principle that states that a conditional statement is logically equivalent to its contrapositive, transforming "If P, then Q" into "If not Q, then not P".

## o220 entry: contrapositive

contrapositive
The statement resulting from swapping the antecedent and consequent of a conditional statement and negating both, maintaining logical equivalence.

## o221 entry: contrary

contrary
Referring to a pair of statements or propositions that cannot both be true, but can both be false.

## o222 entry: convention T

convention T
A criterion of truth proposed by Alfred Tarski, stating that a sentence is true if and only if it corresponds to the facts or reality it describes.

## o223 entry: converse

converse
The statement formed by reversing the antecedent and consequent of a conditional statement, not necessarily maintaining logical equivalence.

## o224 entry: converse domain

converse domain
In set theory and logic, the set of all elements that are related to any element of a given set under a specific relation.

## o225 citation: LemmonLogic

## o226 entry: converse barcan formula

converse barcan formula
A principle in modal logic suggesting that possibility precedes existence, the converse of the Barcan formula.

## o227 entry: conversion

conversion
The logical operation of swapping the subject and predicate of a categorical proposition, sometimes affecting its truth.

## o228 entry: copula

copula
The part of a proposition that links the subject to the predicate, typically represented by forms of the verb "to be".

## o229 entry: co-referential

co-referential
Referring to different expressions that denote the same object or entity in the world.

## o230 entry: coreflexivity

coreflexivity
A property of a binary relation where every element is related to itself, implying reflexivity.

## o231 entry: corollary

corollary
A proposition that follows directly from another proposition or theorem with little or no additional proof.

## o232 entry: correspondence theory of truth

correspondence theory of truth
The philosophical doctrine that the truth or falsity of a statement is determined by how it relates to the world and whether it accurately describes (corresponds with) that world.

## o233 entry: counterexample

counterexample
(broadly) An example that disproves a statement or proposition, showing that it is not universally true.
(to an argument form) A counterexample to an argument form, or sequent, is an argument in the same logical form where the premises are clearly true but the conclusion is clearly false, showing that the form is invalid, since it lacks semantic validity.

## o234 citation: AllenHand

## o235 entry: counterfactual conditional

counterfactual conditional
A conditional statement (if...then...) concerning an event that did not actually happen but is considered for the sake of argument.

## o236 entry: counterfactual logic

counterfactual logic
A branch of logic that studies counterfactual conditionals and their implications, often used in philosophical discussions about causality and decision theory.

## o237 citation

## o238 citation

## o239 citation

## o240 entry: countermodel

countermodel
A countermodel of an argument is a model in which the premises are true and the conclusion false, showing that the argument is not valid.

## o241 citation

## o242 citation: GodelIntro

## o243 entry: counternecessary conditional

counternecessary conditional
A conditional statement that considers a situation against a necessarily true backdrop, exploring implications in hypothetical scenarios that contradict necessary truths; also known as counterpossible.

## o244 citation

## o245 entry: counterpart theory

counterpart theory
A philosophical theory proposed by Lewis that addresses the semantics of modal logic, suggesting that objects in possible worlds have counterparts in other possible worlds.

## o246 entry: course of values recursion

course of values recursion
A principle in mathematics and logic that defines a function based on the values it takes on smaller arguments, essential for defining functions like factorials and other recursive functions.

## o247 entry: Curry paradox

Curry paradox
A paradox in logic that arises when considering a statement that asserts its own unprovability, leading to contradictions in certain systems of formal logic.

## o248 entry: cut

cut
In proof theory, a rule or step in a deductive proof that introduces an intermediate conclusion, which is later used to derive further conclusions.

## o249 entry: cut elimination

cut elimination
A procedure in proof theory that systematically removes cuts from a proof, simplifying it and showing that any result that can be proved with cuts can also be proved without them.

## o250 section: D

## o251 entry: dagger

dagger
A name for the joint denial, or logical NOR.

## o252 citation

## o253 entry: decidable theory

decidable theory
A theory for which there exists a decision procedure, meaning that for any statement within the theory, it is possible to algorithmically determine whether the statement is true or false within the theory.

## o254 entry: decision procedure

decision procedure
An algorithm or systematic method that can decide whether given statements are theorems (true) or non-theorems (false) in a logical system or mathematical theory.

## o255 entry: de dicto

de dicto
Referring to the way a statement attributes a property to a noun phrase as a whole, often contrasted with de re, which attributes a property to the thing itself.

## o256 entry: deduction

deduction
The process of reasoning from one or more statements (premises) to reach a logically certain conclusion.

## o257 entry: deduction theorem

deduction theorem
A theorem stating that if a statement can be derived from a set of premises together with another statement, then the conclusion can be derived from the premises alone by adding the statement as a conditional.

## o258 entry: deductive argument

deductive argument
An argument where the conclusion necessarily follows from the premises, intended to provide conclusive proof of the conclusion.

## o259 entry: deductive consequence

deductive consequence
See syntactic consequence.

## o260 citation: IEP_Compactness

## o261 entry: deductive validity

deductive validity
The property of a deductive argument where, if the premises are true, the conclusion must also be true.
Proof-theoretic validity, where a proof follows from the rules of the formal system. (See proof-theoretic semantics.)

## o262 citation

## o263 citation: SEP_ProofTheoreticSemantics

## o264 entry: deductively equivalent

deductively equivalent
Two theories are deductively equivalent if they prove the same theorems.

## o265 citation

## o266 citation

## o267 citation

## o268 entry: deductivism

deductivism
The philosophical stance that emphasizes deduction as the primary method of reasoning or acquiring knowledge.

## o269 entry: definiendum

definiendum
The term or phrase that is being defined in a definition.

## o270 entry: definiens

definiens
The words or phrases that are used to provide the definition of the definiendum.

## o271 entry: definite description

definite description
A phrase that uniquely identifies a particular individual or concept, often using "the" to specify uniqueness.

## o272 entry: definition

definition
A statement that explains the meaning of a term or concept, providing its essence or specifying its characteristics.

## o273 entry: deflationism

deflationism
A theory of truth that argues the role of the term "true" is merely to allow the expression of propositions that cannot be expressed otherwise, without implying a substantive property of truth.

## o274 entry: degree-theoretic semantics

degree-theoretic semantics
An approach in semantics where the truth of sentences is measured in degrees, rather than as strictly true or false, applicable in fuzzy logic and some theories of vagueness.

## o275 citation

## o276 citation

## o277 citation

## o278 entry: demonstration

demonstration
See proof.

## o279 entry: demonstrative

demonstrative
A word that indicates what object is being referred to, such as "this", "that", "him", "her", and "they".

## o280 entry: De Morgan duality

De Morgan duality
The principle stating that the negation of a conjunction is equivalent to the disjunction of the negations, and vice versa, reflecting the duality between the logical operators AND and OR.

## o281 entry: De Morgan negation

De Morgan negation
A de Morgan negation is a simple negation satisfying double negation elimination: $\neg A$ is false if and only if $A$ is true, and $\neg A$ is true if and only if $A$ is false.

## o282 citation

## o283 citation

## o284 citation

## o285 entry: De Morgan's rules

De Morgan's rules
Two transformation rules stating that the negation of a conjunction is the disjunction of the negations, and the negation of a disjunction is the conjunction of the negations.

## o286 entry: denotation

denotation
The direct reference or literal meaning of a word or phrase, as opposed to its connotation or implied meaning.

## o287 entry: dense

dense
A relation is dense if, for any two elements, there exists a third element that lies between them according to the relation.

## o288 entry: denumerable

denumerable
Equivalent to countably infinite; a set is denumerable if its elements can be put into a one-to-one correspondence with the natural numbers.

## o289 entry: denying the antecedent

denying the antecedent
A logical fallacy in which the invalid argument form of "If P then Q, not P, therefore not Q" is used.

## o290 entry: deontic modal logic

deontic modal logic
A branch of modal logic concerned with obligation, permission, and related modalities.

## o291 entry: derivable rule

derivable rule
A rule that can be inferred from the axioms and inference rules of a logical system, as opposed to being an axiom or primitive rule of the system.

## o292 citation

## o293 citation

## o294 citation

## o295 entry: designated value

designated value
A semantic value that plays the same role for logical consequence as the role played by truth in classical logic, so that, for the consequence to hold, a designated value must be assigned to the conclusion if it is assigned to the premises.

## o296 citation

## o297 citation

## o298 citation

## o299 entry: destructive dilemma

destructive dilemma
A form of argument involving two conditional statements and their negated consequents, leading to the negation of at least one of the antecedents.

## o300 entry: determiner

determiner
A word, phrase, or affix that specifies the reference of a noun or noun phrase, such as "the", "some", "every".

## o301 entry: deterministic polynomial time

deterministic polynomial time
A complexity class (P) consisting of decision problems that can be solved by a deterministic Turing machine in polynomial time.

## o302 citation

## o303 citation

## o304 citation

## o305 entry: deterministic Turing machine

deterministic Turing machine
A theoretical computing machine that, for any given state and input symbol, has a single defined transition to a new state.

## o306 entry: deviant logic

deviant logic
A term used to describe non-standard or alternative logical systems that deviate from classical logic.

## o307 entry: diagonalization lemma

diagonalization lemma
A lemma used in the proof of Gödel's incompleteness theorems, stating that for any formula with one free variable, there exists a sentence that asserts its own unprovability.

## o308 entry: dialetheism

dialetheism
The belief that there are true contradictions, i.e., statements that are both true and false at the same time.

## o309 entry: dialethic logic

dialethic logic
A logic that allows for the existence of true contradictions, challenging the principle of non-contradiction.

## o310 entry: dilemma

dilemma
A situation in which a difficult choice must be made between two or more alternatives, often equally undesirable.

## o311 entry: dilution

dilution
In logic, the weakening of a statement by adding disjunctive clauses that do not affect the original statement's truth.

## o312 entry: directed

directed
A relation is directed if, for every pair of elements, there is a third element related to both, ensuring a kind of coherence or consistency within the set.

## o313 entry: discharge

discharge
The act of eliminating an assumption in a logical derivation, often by using it to prove a conditional statement.

## o314 citation

## o315 citation

## o316 citation

## o317 entry: disjunct

disjunct
One of the component propositions in a disjunction, each of which is an alternative to the others.

## o318 entry: disjunction

disjunction
A logical connective that represents the "or" relationship between two statements, requiring at least one to be true.

## o319 entry: disjunction elimination

disjunction elimination
A rule of inference that allows deriving a conclusion from a disjunction and conditional statements—if $P \lor Q$ and both $P \rightarrow R$ and $Q \rightarrow R$, then $R$.

## o320 entry: disjunction introduction

disjunction introduction
A logical principle allowing the introduction of a disjunction from any single proposition— from $P$, infer $P \lor Q$.

## o321 entry: disjunction property

disjunction property
A property in intuitionistic logic stating that if a disjunction $P \lor Q$ is provable, then either $P$ is provable or $Q$ is provable.

## o322 entry: disjunctive normal form

disjunctive normal form
A standardization of logical formulae in which a formula is expressed as a disjunction of conjunctive clauses.

## o323 entry: disjunctive syllogism

disjunctive syllogism
A form of deductive reasoning that concludes one disjunct must be false if the other is true and a disjunction is given (if $P \lor Q$ and not $P$, then $Q$).

## o324 entry: disquotationalism

disquotationalism
A theory of truth that focuses on the disquotation principle, which suggests that the function of the truth predicate is to remove quotation marks to form equivalent sentences.

## o325 entry: distribution

distribution
A term is distributed within a categorical proposition if, and only if, that proposition says something about all objects instantiating that term.

## o326 entry: distribution axiom

distribution axiom
In modal logic, the axiom (called K) that $\square(p \to q) \to (\square p \to \square q)$.
In epistemic modal logic, the axiom (also called K) that $(K_i\varphi \land K_i(\varphi \implies \psi)) \implies K_i\psi$.

## o327 citation: SEP_ModalLogic

## o328 entry: distributive laws

distributive laws
See distributivity.

## o329 entry: distributive predication

distributive predication
A property of predicates in logic that allows them to be applied to each element of a subject class individually rather than to the class as a whole.

## o330 citation

## o331 citation

## o332 entry: distributivity

distributivity
The characteristic of certain binary operations to be able to distribute over others in a specific algebraic structure, maintaining equivalence.

## o333 entry: division

division
See fallacy of division.

## o334 entry: domain

domain
The set of all possible inputs for a function, or more generally, the subject matter or universe of discourse in a logical argument.

## o335 entry: domain of discourse

domain of discourse
The collection of objects being discussed in a particular logical context, which determines the range of quantifiers.

## o336 entry: dominant connective

dominant connective
The connective with the largest scope in a formula.

## o337 citation

## o338 citation

## o339 entry: double negation

double negation
The application of negation twice to a proposition, which in classical logic results in the original proposition ($\neg \neg P \equiv P$).

## o340 entry: double negation elimination

double negation elimination
A principle in classical logic stating that the double negation of a statement can be removed without changing its truth value ($\neg \neg P$ is equivalent to $P$).

## o341 entry: double negation introduction

double negation introduction
The principle that for any proposition $P$, $P$ implies $\neg \neg P$, reinforcing the assertion of $P$ by denying its negation.

## o342 entry: double turnstile

double turnstile
A symbol ($\models$) used in logic to denote semantic entailment or logical consequence, indicating that the truth of some propositions necessitates the truth of another.

## o343 entry: downward Löwenheim–Skolem theorem

downward Löwenheim–Skolem theorem
Part of the Löwenheim–Skolem theorem.

## o344 entry: doxastic modal logic

doxastic modal logic
A branch of modal logic that deals with the logic of belief, modeling the beliefs of rational agents.

## o345 entry: dual

dual
The dual of a truth-table is obtained by interchanging the truth values "true" and "false" (or 0 and 1) throughout the table. Connectives are dual if their truth-tables are dual: conjunction and disjunction are dual, and negation is self-dual. The dual of a formula is obtained by replacing each connective by its dual, e.g., for a formula containing only conjunction, disjunction, and negation (such as a formula in disjunctive normal form), its dual is the result of replacing each conjunction with a disjunction, and each disjunction with a conjunction. (For a formula in disjunctive normal form, its dual is a formula in conjunctive normal form.)

## o346 citation: OmodeoComputability

## o347 citation

## o348 citation

## o349 citation

## o350 entry: dynamic modal logic

dynamic modal logic
A branch of modal logic that studies necessary and possible connections between events.

## o351 citation

## o352 citation

## o353 section: E

## o354 entry: effective procedure

effective procedure
A method or process that guarantees a solution to a particular problem or class of problems, typically through a finite number of steps that can be precisely followed.

## o355 entry: effectively computable function

effectively computable function
A function for which there exists an algorithm or mechanical procedure that can compute the function's value for any valid input in a finite amount of time.

## o356 entry: effectively decidable relation

effectively decidable relation
A binary relation for which there exists a mechanical method to determine, for any given pair of elements, whether the relation holds between them.

## o357 citation

## o358 citation

## o359 entry: effectively decidable theory

effectively decidable theory
A theory in which there exists an algorithm capable of determining whether any given statement within the theory is true or false.

## o360 citation

## o361 entry: elementary equivalence

elementary equivalence
A relationship between two structures in logic and mathematics where they satisfy the same first-order sentences.

## o362 entry: elementary topos

elementary topos
A concept in category theory generalizing set theory concepts within a more abstract framework, allowing for the definition of mathematical structures in different contexts.

## o363 entry: elimination of quantifiers

elimination of quantifiers
A process in logical deduction where quantifiers are removed from logical expressions while preserving equivalence, often used in the theory of real closed fields.

## o364 entry: elimination rule

elimination rule
A rule in logical inference that allows the derivation of simpler formulas from more complex ones, often by removing logical connectives or quantifiers.

## o365 entry: empty concept

empty concept
A concept that does not have any instantiation in reality or does not refer to any existing object or group of objects.

## o366 citation

## o367 citation

## o368 entry: endomorphism

endomorphism
A function from a mathematical object to itself that preserves the object's structure.

## o369 entry: entailment

entailment
The logical relationship where the truth of one set of statements (the premises) necessitates the truth of another statement (the conclusion).

## o370 entry: enthymeme

enthymeme
A rhetorical argument that is presented with one or more premises left implied rather than explicitly stated.

## o371 entry: Entscheidungsproblem

Entscheidungsproblem
The decision problem, a challenge posed by David Hilbert asking for an algorithm to determine the truth or falsity of any given mathematical statement. The problem was proven to be unsolvable by Alan Turing and Alonzo Church.

## o372 entry: Epimenides paradox

Epimenides paradox
A self-referential paradox involving a statement made by Epimenides, a Cretan, who stated that all Cretans are liars, leading to a logical contradiction if taken to be true.

## o373 entry: epimorphism

epimorphism
A morphism in category theory that is right-cancellable, meaning it behaves like a surjective function in the context of sets.

## o374 entry: epistemic constraint

epistemic constraint
The principle that truths must be knowable. It is brought up in the context of verificationism and Fitch's paradox of knowability.

## o375 citation

## o376 citation

## o377 entry: epistemic modal logic

epistemic modal logic
A branch of modal logic that deals with reasoning about knowledge and belief, using modalities to express what is known and what is believed.

## o378 entry: epistemic paradox

epistemic paradox
A paradox arising from basic intuitions regarding knowledge, belief, or related epistemic notions. For instance, the knower paradox and the Fitch paradox.

## o379 entry: epistemic vagueness

epistemic vagueness
The view that vagueness is a feature of human knowledge, rather than of the world or of language. Contrast in rebus vagueness and semantic vagueness.

## o380 citation: VaguenessGuide

## o381 entry: epistemicism

epistemicism
The philosophical position that there are truths that cannot be known, typically applied to discussions of vagueness and the sorites paradox.

## o382 entry: E-proposition

E-proposition
In traditional logic, a universal negative categorical proposition, stating that no members of one class are members of another class.

## o383 citation

## o384 entry: equivalence

equivalence
See deductively equivalent, logically equivalent, materially equivalent.

## o385 entry: equivalence relation

equivalence relation
A binary relation that is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive, indicating that elements it relates are in some sense "equivalent."

## o386 entry: equivalent

equivalent
See deductively equivalent, logically equivalent, materially equivalent.

## o387 entry: equivocation

equivocation
A logical fallacy involving the use of a word with more than one meaning throughout an argument, leading to a misleading or unsound conclusion.

## o388 entry: erotetic logic

erotetic logic
The logic of questions, including the study of the forms and principles of questions and their relationships to answers.

## o389 entry: Eubulides paradox

Eubulides paradox
A paradox presented by Eubulides of Miletus, including the liar paradox, which involves a statement declaring itself to be false, creating a contradiction.

## o390 entry: Euclidean

Euclidean
A relation R where, for any objects x, y, and z, it is true that if Rxy and Rxz, then Ryz.

## o391 entry: exchange

exchange
See permutation.

## o392 entry: excluded middle

excluded middle
The principle that for any proposition, either that proposition is true or its negation is true, with no middle ground.

## o393 entry: exclusion negation

exclusion negation
In three-valued logic, form of negation that strictly excludes the possibility of something being true, as opposed to constructive negation which asserts the truth of an opposite proposition.

## o394 citation

## o395 citation

## o396 entry: exclusive disjunction

exclusive disjunction
A logical operation that is true if and only if exactly one of its operands is true, also known as "xor" or "exclusive or."

## o397 entry: exclusive or

exclusive or
A binary logical operation that returns true only when the inputs are different; symbolized as XOR or $\oplus$.

## o398 entry: ex falso quodlibet

ex falso quodlibet
A principle in classical logic stating that from a falsehood, any conclusion can be derived, also known as the principle of explosion.

## o399 entry: existence predicate

existence predicate
A predicate, usually occurring in free logics, that asserts the existence of the referent of a constant.

## o400 citation

## o401 citation

## o402 entry: existential generalization

existential generalization
A rule of inference allowing the conclusion that something exists with a certain property, based on the existence of a particular example.

## o403 entry: existential import

existential import
The implication that something exists by the assertion of a particular kind of statement, especially relevant in traditional syllogistic logic.

## o404 entry: existential instantiation

existential instantiation
A logical rule that allows one to infer the existence of a particular individual from a statement asserting the existence of such an individual generically.

## o405 entry: existential introduction

existential introduction
A rule of inference that introduces an existential quantifier, asserting the existence of at least one entity that satisfies a given property.

## o406 entry: existential quantifier

existential quantifier
A quantifier used in predicate logic to indicate that there exists at least one member of the domain for which the predicate holds true.

## o407 entry: existential variable

existential variable
A variable in predicate logic that is bound by an existential quantifier, representing an unspecified member of the domain that satisfies the predicate.

## o408 citation

## o409 citation

## o410 entry: explanandum

explanandum
The statement or phenomenon that is being explained in an explanation.

## o411 entry: explanans

explanans
The statement or set of statements that provide the explanation for the phenomenon or statement referred to by the explanandum.

## o412 entry: explanation

explanation
The act of clarifying, elucidating, or making something understandable through detailing reasons, causes, or justifications.

## o413 entry: explicit definition

explicit definition
A definition that provides a means for replacing each occurrence of the definiendum with an appropriate instance of the definiens. Contrast implicit definition.

## o414 citation: Springer_Implicit

## o415 citation

## o416 entry: explosion

explosion
The principle in logic that from a contradiction, any statement can be proven, related to the principle of ex falso quodlibet.

## o417 entry: exportation

exportation
A logical principle that transforms a statement of the form $(P \land Q) \rightarrow R$ into the equivalent form $P \rightarrow (Q \rightarrow R)$, essentially redistributing the logical operations.

## o418 citation

## o419 entry: expressive completeness

expressive completeness
Synonym of functional completeness.

## o420 entry: extension

extension
The collection of objects or entities to which a term or concept applies, contrasted with its intension, which refers to the properties or characteristics defining those objects or entities.

## o421 entry: extensional logic

extensional logic
A form of logic where the truth of sentences and arguments depends solely on the extension of the terms involved, disregarding their intension or conceptual content.

## o422 section: F

## o423 entry: factivity

factivity
The property of certain expressions or verbs that implies the truth of the propositions they refer to, often used in the context of knowledge and belief.

## o424 citation

## o425 citation

## o426 entry: fallacy

fallacy
An error in reasoning that results in an invalid argument or one that is not logically sound.

## o427 entry: fallacy of composition

fallacy of composition
The logical error of assuming that what is true of the parts must also be true of the whole.

## o428 entry: fallacy of division

fallacy of division
The logical error of assuming that what is true of the whole must also be true of its parts.

## o429 entry: fallacy of four terms

fallacy of four terms
A logical fallacy in syllogistic logic where a syllogism includes four (rather than the requisite three) distinct terms, making the argument invalid.

## o430 entry: false dichotomy

false dichotomy
An informal fallacy that presents two options as the only possibilities when in fact more possibilities exist.

## o431 entry: false dilemma

false dilemma
A form of false dichotomy where two choices are presented as the only options when other alternatives are available, often used to force a decision.

## o432 entry: falsemaker

falsemaker
An entity or set of conditions that makes a proposition false. In metaphysics, it's considered the counterpart to a truthmaker, which validates the truth of a proposition.

## o433 entry: falsity

falsity
The quality or condition of being false or untrue.

## o434 entry: falsum

falsum
A symbol used in logic to represent falsity or a contradiction, often denoted as $\bot$.

## o435 entry: "Fido"-Fido principle

"Fido"-Fido principle
The principle in philosophy of language suggesting that the meaning of a word is the object it refers to, exemplified by the idea that the meaning of "Fido" is the dog Fido itself.

## o436 citation

## o437 entry: field

field
The field of a function is the union of the domain and range of that function.

## o438 entry: figure

figure
See syllogistic figure.

## o439 entry: finitary

finitary
Pertaining to methods or processes that involve a finite number of steps or elements.

## o440 entry: finitary arithmetic

finitary arithmetic
An approach to arithmetic focusing on finitary methods, avoiding infinities and emphasizing constructions that can be completed in a finite number of steps.

## o441 citation

## o442 entry: finitary formal system

finitary formal system
A formal system in which all operations, proofs, and expressions are finitary, relying only on objects that can be constructed or demonstrated in a finite number of steps.

## o443 citation

## o444 citation

## o445 entry: finite character

finite character
The property of certain mathematical or logical systems where every relevant feature or property can be determined by examining only a finite part of the system.

## o446 entry: finite model theory

finite model theory
A branch of model theory that deals with the study of properties of structures (models) that have a finite domain.

## o447 entry: finitely axiomatizable

finitely axiomatizable
Referring to a theory that can be fully captured by a finite set of axioms.

## o448 citation

## o449 citation

## o450 citation

## o451 entry: finitism

finitism
A philosophical view that rejects the existence of infinite entities and infinite processes, emphasizing only those quantities or procedures that are finite.

## o452 entry: first-degree entailment (FDE)

first-degree entailment (FDE)
A logical system that allows for the existence of both true and false atomic propositions but does not require every proposition to be either true or false, rejecting the law of the excluded middle for certain propositions.

## o453 citation

## o454 citation

## o455 entry: first-order logic

first-order logic
A formal logical system involving quantifiers "for all" and "there exists," which can quantify over individuals but not over predicates or functions.

## o456 entry: first-order theory

first-order theory
A theory expressed in first-order logic, dealing with the relationships between individuals and properties or relations that individuals may have.

## o457 entry: first-order variable

first-order variable
A variable in first-order logic that represents an individual in the domain of discourse.

## o458 entry: Fitch paradox

Fitch paradox
A paradox in epistemic logic that arises from seemingly reasonable assumptions about knowledge and leads to the conclusion that all truths are known.

## o459 entry: fixed point

fixed point
In mathematics and logic, a value or element that is mapped to itself by a particular function or operation.

## o460 entry: forced march sorites

forced march sorites
A type of sorites paradox involving a series of incremental steps or changes that lead to a contradiction, challenging the precision of vague predicates by forcing a march from one end of a spectrum to another.

## o461 citation

## o462 citation

## o463 entry: formal consequence

formal consequence
A conclusion that follows in virtue of logical form alone, as distinguished from a material consequence, which (also) depends on the content of the propositions. See also syntactic consequence.

## o464 citation: Ashworth_Formal_Material

## o465 citation: Gabbay_Formal_Material

## o466 citation: Buridan_Formal_Material

## o467 entry: formal fallacy

formal fallacy
A logical error in the form or structure of an argument, as opposed to a material fallacy which relates to the argument's content.

## o468 entry: formal language

formal language
A set of strings of symbols that are constructed according to specific syntactic rules, used in mathematics, computer science, and formal logic to precisely define expressions without ambiguity.

## o469 entry: formal logic

formal logic
The study of inference with purely formal content, where no interpretation is given to the terms and only the logical form is considered.

## o470 entry: formal proof

formal proof
A proof in which each step is justified by a rule of inference, constructed within a formal system to demonstrate the truth of a proposition.

## o471 entry: formal semantics

formal semantics
The study of how formal systems, especially those used in logic and linguistics, are connected to conceptual or referential interpretations.

## o472 entry: formal system

formal system
A system of symbols and rules for manipulating these symbols, used to derive statements or theorems in a logical or mathematical domain.

## o473 entry: formation rules

formation rules
Rules that specify the correct ways in which the basic symbols of a formal language can be combined to form well-formed formulas.

## o474 entry: formula

formula
An expression in a formal language that can be evaluated as true or false within a given interpretation, often involving variables and logical connectives.

## o475 entry: frame

frame
In modal logic, a structure consisting of a set of possible worlds and a relation between those worlds, used to interpret modal propositions.

## o476 entry: frame semantics

frame semantics
A theory in linguistics and logic that uses frames—conceptual structures for representing stereotypical situations—as a means of understanding how language conveys meaning.

## o477 entry: free choice sequence

free choice sequence
A sequence (typically of natural numbers) where each term is chosen freely, not determined by any rule or algorithm, often used in discussions of constructivism and intuitionism.

## o478 entry: free logic

free logic
A form of logic that allows for terms that do not denote any existing object, differing from classical logic by not requiring every term to refer to something in the domain of discourse.

## o479 entry: free variable

free variable
A variable in a formula that is not bound by a quantifier and does not have a specific value assigned to it within the context of the formula.

## o480 entry: Frege's theorem

Frege's theorem
A result in logic and mathematics demonstrating that arithmetic can be derived from logic through the introduction of the concept of a successor and the use of second-order quantification.

## o481 entry: function

function
A relation between sets that associates every element of a first set with exactly one element of a second set, often represented as a mapping from elements of one set to elements of another.

## o482 entry: fuzzy logic

fuzzy logic
A many-valued logic which allows for degrees of truth and certainty.

## o483 section: G

## o484 entry: gambler's fallacy

gambler's fallacy
The erroneous belief that if an event occurs more frequently than normal during the past, it is less likely to happen in the future (or vice versa), often arising in contexts of gambling and misinterpretation of statistics.

## o485 entry: game-theoretic semantics

game-theoretic semantics
An approach to semantics that interprets the meaning of linguistic expressions through the outcomes of certain idealized games played between a verifier and a falsifier, emphasizing the interactive process of establishing truth or falsehood.

## o486 citation

## o487 entry: gap

gap
See truth-value gap.

## o488 entry: Geach-Kaplan sentence

Geach-Kaplan sentence
A type of sentence that raises issues in the philosophy of language and logic regarding context-dependence, referential opacity, and the limits of formal semantic analysis. Named after philosophers Peter Geach and David Kaplan.

## o489 entry: generalized quantifier

generalized quantifier
In logic and linguistics, a quantifier that can express more complex relationships than standard quantifiers like "all" or "some," allowing for the expression of concepts like "most," "many," and "few."

## o490 entry: Glivenko's theorem

Glivenko's theorem
A result in logic stating that if a formula is provable in classical logic, then its double negation is provable in intuitionistic logic, establishing a connection between the two logics.

## o491 entry: glut

glut
See truth-value glut.

## o492 entry: Gödel numbering

Gödel numbering
A method of encoding mathematical and logical symbols and expressions as natural numbers, introduced by Kurt Gödel as part of his incompleteness theorems.

## o493 entry: Gödel sentence

Gödel sentence
A self-referential sentence constructed in formal systems to demonstrate Gödel's incompleteness theorems, asserting its own unprovability within the system.

## o494 entry: Gödel-Dummett logic

Gödel-Dummett logic
A form of intuitionistic logic that includes a principle of maximal elements, allowing for the expression of certain intermediate truth values between true and false.

## o495 entry: Gödel's first incompleteness theorem

Gödel's first incompleteness theorem
A theorem proving that in any consistent formal system that is capable of expressing basic arithmetic, there are propositions that cannot be proven or disproven within the system.

## o496 entry: Gödel's second incompleteness theorem

Gödel's second incompleteness theorem
A theorem establishing that no consistent system capable of doing arithmetic can prove its own consistency, building on the first incompleteness theorem.

## o497 entry: Gödel's slingshot argument

Gödel's slingshot argument
An argument concerning the semantics of reference and truth, challenging the coherence of theories that attempt to distinguish between facts and true propositions in a fine-grained manner.

## o498 entry: Grelling paradox

Grelling paradox
A paradox related to self-reference and linguistic categories, particularly whether the word "heterological," meaning not applicable to itself, applies to itself.

## o499 section: H

## o500 entry: halting problem

halting problem
The decision problem of determining, from a description of an arbitrary computer program and an input, whether the program will finish running or continue to run forever. Proven to be undecidable by Alan Turing.

## o501 entry: harmony

harmony
In logic and philosophy, the notion that the introduction and elimination rules for a logical connective should be in balance, ensuring that the connective does not introduce more than it can eliminate, and vice versa.

## o502 entry: hasty generalization

hasty generalization
An informal fallacy in which a conclusion is not logically justified by sufficient or unbiased evidence; drawing a general conclusion from a too-small sample size.

## o503 entry: Henkin semantics

Henkin semantics
A generalization of standard first-order semantics that allows for models where the range of quantifiers can be restricted, named after Leon Henkin.

## o504 entry: Henkin sentence

Henkin sentence
A sentence that asserts its own provability, possibly by applying the provability predicate to itself.

## o505 citation

## o506 citation

## o507 entry: hereditary property

hereditary property
A property of objects in a mathematical or logical structure that, if applied to an object, also applies to all subobjects or elements of that object.

## o508 entry: heterological

heterological
Describing an adjective that does not apply to itself. For example, "long" is not long, so "long" is heterological.

## o509 entry: hierarchy

hierarchy
A system in which entities are ranked one above the other based on certain criteria, often used in the context of sets, classes, or organizational structures. In logic, an important one is Tarskian hierarchy. In set theory, an important one is the cumulative hierarchy.

## o510 entry: higher-order logic

higher-order logic
A form of logic that extends first-order logic by allowing quantification over predicates and possibly other higher-order entities, not just individuals.

## o511 entry: higher-order quantifier

higher-order quantifier
A quantifier that binds a higher-order variable.

## o512 citation

## o513 citation

## o514 entry: higher-order vagueness

higher-order vagueness
Vagueness about the application of the concept of vagueness itself, particularly in the context of predicates that are borderline cases of borderline cases.

## o515 citation

## o516 entry: higher-order variable

higher-order variable
A variable in higher-order logic that represents a function, predicate, or relation, rather than an individual object.

## o517 citation

## o518 citation

## o519 entry: Hilbert's program

Hilbert's program
An ambitious project proposed by David Hilbert to provide a solid foundation for all of mathematics by formalizing it and proving it consistent using finitary methods.

## o520 entry: holism

holism
The idea that systems and their properties should be analyzed as wholes, not just as a collection of parts, often discussed in the context of meaning, knowledge, and the philosophy of science.

## o521 entry: homomorphism

homomorphism
A structure-preserving map between two algebraic structures of the same type, such as groups, rings, or vector spaces, that respects the operations of the structures.

## o522 entry: horn

horn
Refers to either of the two alternatives presented by a dilemma.

## o523 citation

## o524 citation

## o525 entry: Hume's principle

Hume's principle
The principle that the number of objects in one collection is equal to the number of objects in another collection if and only if there is a one-to-one correspondence between the two collections.

## o526 entry: hybrid logic

hybrid logic
A type of modal logic that incorporates additional syntactic elements to refer directly to worlds in its models, allowing for more expressive power than standard modal logics.

## o527 entry: hypothetical syllogism

hypothetical syllogism
A form of logical argument consisting of three propositions: two conditional statements and a conclusion that infers a relationship between the antecedent of the first conditional and the consequent of the second.

## o528 section: I

## o529 entry: idempotent

idempotent
A property of certain operations in which applying the operation multiple times has the same effect as applying it once. For example, the union of a set with itself is the set itself.

## o530 entry: identity

identity
The relation that each entity bears only to itself; or, the principle that an entity is the same as itself.

## o531 entry: identity function

identity function
A function that always returns its input as its output, for any given input.

## o532 entry: identity of indiscernibles

identity of indiscernibles
A principle stating that if two entities share all their properties, they must be identical.

## o533 entry: iff

iff
An abbreviation for "if and only if," denoting a biconditional logical connective indicating mutual implication.

## o534 entry: ignoratio elenchi

ignoratio elenchi
A logical fallacy where an argument misses the point or addresses an issue different from the one that was raised, also known as missing the point.

## o535 entry: image

image
The set of all outputs of a function from a given set of inputs.

## o536 entry: immediate inference

immediate inference
A process of reasoning in which a conclusion is directly derived from a single premise without any intermediary steps.

## o537 entry: imperative

imperative
A type of linguistic expression or logical form that expresses a command, request, or instruction.

## o538 entry: imperative logic

imperative logic
A branch of logic that studies the formal properties of imperatives or commands, distinct from indicative statements.

## o539 entry: implication

implication
A logical relation where the truth of one statement (the antecedent) brings about the truth of another statement (the consequent).

## o540 entry: implicit definition

implicit definition
A definition that specifies an entity or concept not by direct enumeration of its properties but by its relations to other entities or concepts.

## o541 citation

## o542 entry: impossibility

impossibility
The state or condition of something being not possible, either logically or physically.

## o543 entry: impossible world

impossible world
A hypothetical construct in modal logic representing a way things could not possibly be, used to explore concepts of necessity and possibility.

## o544 entry: impredicative definition

impredicative definition
A definition that refers to a set that includes the entity being defined, often leading to paradoxes in naive set theories.

## o545 entry: inclusive disjunction

inclusive disjunction
A logical operation that returns true if at least one of its operands is true; corresponds to the logical OR.

## o546 entry: inclusive first-order logic

inclusive first-order logic
A variant of first-order logic that allows for empty domains, in contrast to the standard requirement that domains contain at least one object.

## o547 entry: inclusive or

inclusive or
The disjunction operation in logic that is true if either or both of its operands are true.

## o548 entry: incompleteness

incompleteness
The property of a logical or mathematical system where not all truths within the system can be proven using the system's rules.

## o549 entry: incompleteness theorem

incompleteness theorem
Gödel's theorem stating that any consistent formal system capable of expressing basic arithmetic cannot be both complete and consistent.

## o550 entry: inconsistency

inconsistency
The state of containing contradictory elements, which cannot all be true at the same time within a logical framework.

## o551 entry: inconsistent arithmetic

inconsistent arithmetic
An arithmetic system in which a contradiction can be derived, violating the principle of consistency.

## o552 entry: indefinite description

indefinite description
A description that does not uniquely identify a single individual or entity but refers to any member of a class that satisfies a certain condition.

## o553 entry: indefinite extensibility

indefinite extensibility
The concept that certain collections (such as the set of all sets) cannot be comprehensively listed because any attempt to enumerate them leads to the possibility of generating new members.

## o554 citation

## o555 citation

## o556 citation

## o557 entry: independence result

independence result
A finding in logic and mathematics that a particular statement cannot be proven or disproven within a given system, assuming the system's axioms are consistent.

## o558 entry: independence-friendly logic

independence-friendly logic
A logic that extends first-order logic to allow for more nuanced expressions of quantifier scope and dependence, particularly in contexts of game-theoretical semantics.

## o559 entry: independent

independent
Referring to a pair of propositions that are not contrary, subcontrary, contradictory, logically equivalent, or implied one by the other (either the first by the second, or the second by the first).

## o560 citation

## o561 entry: indeterminacy of translation

indeterminacy of translation
Inition proposed by W.V.O. Quine, suggesting that no unique translation between languages can be determined solely by empirical evidence, due to the underdetermination of theories by data.

## o562 entry: indexical

indexical
A type of expression whose reference can shift based on the context in which it is used, such as "I," "here," and "now."

## o563 entry: indicative conditional

indicative conditional
A conditional statement used to express factual implications or predictions about real situations, as opposed to counterfactual or hypothetical statements.

## o564 entry: indirect proof

indirect proof
A method of proof in which the negation of the statement to be proven is assumed, and a contradiction is derived, thereby proving the original statement by contradiction.

## o565 entry: indiscernibility

indiscernibility
The inability to distinguish between objects due to them sharing all properties, related to the principle of identity of indiscernibles.

## o566 entry: indiscernibility of identicals

indiscernibility of identicals
The principle stating that if two entities are identical, then they share all the same properties.

## o567 entry: indispensability argument

indispensability argument
An argument suggesting that if a mathematical entity is indispensable to our best scientific theories, then we ought to commit to its existence.

## o568 entry: individual

individual
An entity regarded as indivisible or atomistic in nature, often discussed in the context of metaphysics, logic, and the philosophy of language.

## o569 entry: induction

induction
See inductive argument, induction on well-formed formulas, mathematical induction, strong mathematical induction, transfinite induction, weak mathematical induction

## o570 entry: induction on well-formed formulas

induction on well-formed formulas
A method used in formal logic and mathematics to prove properties of all well-formed formulas by showing they hold for basic formulas and are preserved under the operations that generate new formulas.

## o571 entry: induction schema

induction schema
Synonym of mathematical induction.

## o572 entry: inductive argument

inductive argument
An argument that provides probable support for its conclusion, as opposed to deductive arguments which provide conclusive support.

## o573 entry: inductive proof

inductive proof
A proof method used in mathematics to prove statements about all natural numbers or other well-ordered sets, based on the principle of induction.

## o574 entry: inductive step

inductive step
In an inductive proof, the step that shows if a property holds for an arbitrary element $n$, it also holds for the next element $n+1$.

## o575 entry: inference

inference
The process of deriving logical conclusions from premises known or assumed to be true.

## o576 entry: inference rule

inference rule
A logical rule that outlines the conditions under which certain conclusions can be validly drawn from premises.

## o577 entry: inference to the best explanation

inference to the best explanation
A form of abductive reasoning that suggests we should accept the hypothesis that best explains the relevant evidence.

## o578 entry: inferentialism

inferentialism
