The philosophical view that the meaning of a statement is determined by the rules governing its use and the inferences that can be drawn from it.

## o579 entry: infinitary

infinitary
Pertaining to operations, languages, or logics that allow expressions of infinite length, such as infinitary logic.

## o580 entry: infinitesimal

infinitesimal
A quantity that is closer to zero than any positive real number, yet not zero, used in non-standard analysis.

## o581 entry: infix notation

infix notation
A notation in which the operator is placed between the operands, as in standard arithmetic expressions.

## o582 entry: informal fallacy

informal fallacy
A flaw in reasoning that occurs in natural language arguments due to ambiguity, irrelevance, or other factors outside the formal structure of the argument.

## o583 entry: injection

injection
A function that maps distinct elements of its domain to distinct elements of its codomain, also known as an injective function or one-to-one function.

## o584 entry: injective

injective
Describing a function in which each element of the codomain is mapped to by at most one element of the domain.

## o585 entry: inner model

inner model
An inner model of a theory is a model that is obtained by taking a substructure of another model.

## o586 entry: in rebus vagueness

in rebus vagueness
The view that vagueness is a feature of the world, rather than of language or of human knowledge. Contrast epistemic vagueness and semantic vagueness. See also ontic vagueness.

## o587 entry: insolubilia

insolubilia
Unsolvable problems or paradoxes, especially those related to self-reference and logical contradiction, such as the liar paradox.

## o588 entry: instantiation

instantiation
The process of replacing bound variables with constants, eliminating the quantifiers that had bound them.

## o589 citation

## o590 citation

## o591 citation

## o592 entry: intended interpretation

intended interpretation
The standard or conventional meaning assigned to the symbols and expressions of a formal language within a particular context or theory.

## o593 entry: intension

intension
The inherent content or essential properties and meanings of a concept or term, as opposed to its extension, which refers to the range of things it applies to.

## o594 entry: intensional definition

intensional definition
A definition that specifies the essential properties of the term being defined, focusing on the concept's intension rather than its extension.

## o595 entry: intensional logic

intensional logic
A logic that deals with the intensional aspects of meaning, such as belief, necessity, and possibility, distinguishing between logically equivalent expressions that have different modal properties.

## o596 entry: intermediate logic

intermediate logic
Any logical system that falls between intuitionistic logic and classical logic in strength, allowing for distinctions not permissible in classical logic.

## o597 entry: interpolation theorem

interpolation theorem
A result stating that if a formula $A \rightarrow B$ is provable, then there exists a formula $C$ containing only the non-logical symbols common to $A$ and $B$ such that $A \rightarrow C$ and $C \rightarrow B$ are both provable.

## o598 entry: interpretation

interpretation
The assignment of meanings to the symbols and expressions of a formal language, or the way a particular set of terms is understood or construed.

## o599 entry: interrogative

interrogative
Pertaining to questions or the act of questioning.

## o600 entry: interrogative logic

interrogative logic
See erotetic logic.

## o601 entry: introduction rule

introduction rule
In logic, a rule that allows for the introduction of a connective into a proof, defining how that connective can be logically inferred.

## o602 entry: intuitionism

intuitionism
A philosophy of mathematics that denies the reality of the mathematical infinite and the completeness of mathematical truth, requiring constructive proofs.

## o603 entry: intuitionistic logic

intuitionistic logic
A system of logic that reflects the principles of intuitionism, rejecting the law of excluded middle and requiring more constructive proofs of existence.

## o604 entry: intuitionistic mathematics

intuitionistic mathematics
Mathematics based on intuitionistic logic, emphasizing constructive methods and rejecting non-constructive principles like the law of excluded middle.

## o605 entry: invalid

invalid
Referring to an argument whose conclusion does not logically follow from its premises.

## o606 entry: invalid deductive argument

invalid deductive argument
A deductive argument that fails to provide conclusive support for its conclusion, due to a flaw in logical structure.

## o607 entry: inverse

inverse
A operation or function that reverses the effect of another operation or function.

## o608 entry: involution

involution
An operation that is its own inverse, meaning applying it twice returns to the original state.

## o609 entry: I-proposition

I-proposition
In traditional logic, a particular affirmative categorical proposition, stating that some members of the subject class are members of the predicate class.

## o610 citation

## o611 entry: irreflexivity

irreflexivity
A property of a relation where no element is related to itself.

## o612 entry: "is" of identity

"is" of identity
The use of the verb "is" to denote numerical identity between two entities.

## o613 citation: VallicellaPredication

## o614 citation: OntologyPredication

## o615 citation: VilkkoPredication

## o616 entry: "is" of predication

"is" of predication
The use of the verb "is" to signify that a property or characteristic is attributed to a subject.

## o617 entry: island of knights and knaves

island of knights and knaves
A fictional scenario used in logic puzzles where inhabitants are either knights, who always tell the truth, or knaves, who always lie, posing challenges to deductive reasoning.

## o618 entry: isomorphism

isomorphism
A bijective (one-to-one and onto) correspondence between two structures that preserves the operations and relations of the structures, indicating they have the same form or structure.

## o619 entry: iteration

iteration
The process of repeating a set of operations or a procedure multiple times, each time applying it to the result of the previous step.

## o620 entry: iteration theorem

iteration theorem
Synonym for the S theorem.

## o621 section: J

## o622 entry: joint denial

joint denial
A logical connective in propositional logic, equivalent to the nor operator, that is true if and only if both propositions it connects are false. It denies the joint assertion of both propositions.

## o623 section: K

## o624 entry: K3

K3
The three-valued logic K3, due to Stephen Cole Kleene.

## o625 entry: Kleene connectives

Kleene connectives
Logical connectives defined using Kleene's three-valued logic, which includes a third truth value (undefined or unknown) in addition to true and false, accommodating indeterminate propositions.

## o626 citation

## o627 entry: knaves

knaves
In logic puzzles, individuals who always lie. Used in scenarios like the island of knights and knaves to explore logical deduction.

## o628 entry: knights

knights
In logic puzzles, individuals who always tell the truth. They are used alongside knaves in scenarios that challenge deductive reasoning.

## o629 entry: knowability paradox

knowability paradox
A paradox arising from the assumption that if a statement is true, then it is possible to know that it is true, leading to contradictions in certain epistemic frameworks.

## o630 entry: knower's paradox

knower's paradox
A paradox related to self-reference and epistemic logic, typically involving a statement that claims its own unprovability or unknowability.

## o631 entry: Kreisel-Putnam logic

Kreisel-Putnam logic
A logic developed to handle higher-order quantification and modalities, reflecting discussions on the foundations of mathematics by Kreisel and Putnam.

## o632 entry: Kripke semantics

Kripke semantics
A framework for interpreting modal logic through the use of possible worlds, developed by Saul Kripke, allowing for the formal analysis of necessity, possibility, and other modal notions.

## o633 entry: Kripke structure

Kripke structure
A mathematical structure used in modal logic and computer science to model systems that can be in various states and transition between them, forming the basis for Kripke semantics.

## o634 section: L

## o635 entry: lambda-calculus

lambda-calculus
A formal system in mathematical logic and computer science for expressing computation based on function abstraction and application, using variable binding and substitution.

## o636 entry: Lambek calculus

Lambek calculus
A mathematical system for modeling the syntax of natural languages, based on categorial grammar, emphasizing the role of operations and types.

## o637 entry: language

language
A system of communication that enables humans to cooperate, express ideas, emotions, and, in the context of formal languages, to construct and convey precise meanings. See formal language, metalanguage, natural language, object language, sublanguage, translation.

## o638 entry: law of non-contradiction

law of non-contradiction
A fundamental principle of classical logic stating that contradictory statements cannot both be true in the same sense at the same time.

## o639 entry: left field

left field
See domain.

## o640 entry: Leibniz's Law

Leibniz's Law
The principle of the identity of indiscernibles, stating that if two entities share all the same properties, then they are identical.

## o641 entry: lemma

lemma
A proposition proved or assumed to be true, used to prove other propositions or theorems.

## o642 entry: liar paradox

liar paradox
A self-referential paradox involving a statement that declares itself to be false, leading to a contradiction if it is either true or false.

## o643 entry: liar sentence

liar sentence
A sentence that asserts its own falsity, such as "This sentence is false," which creates the basis for the liar paradox.

## o644 citation

## o645 entry: limitation result

limitation result
A result that establishes a boundary or limit on what can be achieved within a particular logical or mathematical system, often related to incompleteness or undecidability.

## o646 entry: linear logics

linear logics
A subfield of logic that emphasizes the concept of resources, where logical operations consume their arguments, differing from classical logic's treatment of assumptions as reusable.

## o647 entry: linear order

linear order
A total order on a set where every pair of elements is comparable, meaning for any two elements, one is either greater than, less than, or equal to the other.

## o648 entry: linear ordering

linear ordering
Another term for linear order, emphasizing the arrangement of elements in a sequence where each is comparable to the others in a single, unambiguous way.

## o649 entry: literal

literal
Either a propositional variable (e.g., $P$) or its negation (e.g., $\lnot P$).

## o650 citation

## o651 entry: Löb paradox

Löb paradox
A paradox in modal logic that arises from attempting to formalize a statement's provability within the system, leading to conclusions that appear counterintuitive or self-contradictory.

## o652 entry: Löb's theorem

Löb's theorem
A theorem in mathematical logic that provides conditions under which a statement about its own provability is provable, related to Gödel's incompleteness theorems.

## o653 entry: logic

logic
The systematic study of the form of valid inference, including the structures that allow or compel particular conclusions given certain premises.

## o654 entry: logic gate

logic gate
A physical device implementing a Boolean function, used in digital circuits to perform logical operations on one or more binary inputs to produce a single binary output.

## o655 entry: logical machine

logical machine
A mechanical device for performing formal logic such as the Stanhope Demonstrator or Jevon's logic piano.

## o656 entry: logic of attributes

logic of attributes
See monadic first-order logic.

## o657 entry: logic of conditionals

logic of conditionals
See conditional logic.

## o658 entry: Logic of Paradox

Logic of Paradox
A logical system that allows for some contradictions to be true, challenging the traditional law of non-contradiction and exploring the consequences of paradoxical statements.

## o659 entry: logic of questions and answers

logic of questions and answers
See erotetic logic.

## o660 entry: logic of relations

logic of relations
A branch of logic that deals with the study of relations, including their properties, composition, and inversion, and how they interact with logical operators.

## o661 entry: logic of weak excluded middle

logic of weak excluded middle
An intermediate logic obtained by adding all instances of weak excluded middle to propositional logic. Also called "KC" and "Jankov's logic".

## o662 citation

## o663 citation: Bezhanishvili

## o664 entry: logical antirealism

logical antirealism
The philosophical position that logical truths do not correspond to an independent reality but are instead products of human conventions, language, or thought processes.

## o665 entry: logical connective

logical connective
A symbol or word used in logic to connect propositions or sentences, forming more complex expressions that convey relationships such as conjunction, disjunction, and negation.

## o666 entry: logical consequence

logical consequence
A relationship between statements where the truth of one or more premises necessitates the truth of a conclusion, based on the logical structure of the statements. See semantic consequence and syntactic consequence.

## o667 entry: logical constant

logical constant
A symbol in logic that has the same meaning in all interpretations, such as connectives and quantifiers, as opposed to variables whose interpretations can vary.

## o668 entry: logical equivalence

logical equivalence
The relationship between statements that are true under exactly the same conditions, allowing them to be substituted for one another in logical proofs.

## o669 entry: logical falsehood

logical falsehood
A statement that is false under all possible interpretations, also known as a contradiction.

## o670 citation

## o671 entry: logical form

logical form
The abstract structure of a statement or argument, representing the logical relationships between its components independent of the specific content.

## o672 entry: logical implication

logical implication
The logical relationship that holds between propositions when the truth of one (the antecedent) guarantees the truth of another (the consequent).

## o673 entry: logical monism

logical monism
The philosophical position that there is only one correct logic or logical system that accurately captures the principles of valid reasoning.

## o674 citation: SEP_LogicalPluralism

## o675 entry: logical operator

logical operator
A symbol or function in logic that applies to one or more propositions, producing another proposition that expresses a logical operation such as negation, conjunction, or disjunction.

## o676 entry: logical paradox

logical paradox
A statement or group of statements that lead to a contradiction or a situation that defies intuition, often highlighting limitations or problems within the logical system. Sometimes distinguished from semantic paradox.

## o677 entry: logical pluralism

logical pluralism
The view that there are multiple equally correct logics or systems of logic that capture different aspects of reasoning or argumentation.

## o678 citation

## o679 entry: logical structure

logical structure
See logical form.

## o680 entry: logical theorem

logical theorem
A proposition that has been proven to be true within a specific system of logic, based on the system's axioms and inference rules.

## o681 entry: logical truth

logical truth
A statement that is true in all possible worlds or under all possible interpretations, due to its logical form rather than the content of its terms.

## o682 entry: logical validity

logical validity
The property of an argument wherein if the premises are true, the conclusion necessarily follows, due to the structure of the argument rather than the specific nature of the premises or conclusion.

## o683 entry: logically equivalent

logically equivalent
Referring to statements that have the same truth value in every possible scenario, indicating that they are interchangeable in logical reasoning.

## o684 entry: logicism

logicism
The philosophical belief that mathematics can be reduced to logic and that all mathematical truths can be derived from logical axioms and definitions.

## o685 entry: Löwenheim–Skolem theorem

Löwenheim–Skolem theorem
A theorem in mathematical logic that states any countable theory with an infinite model has models of all infinite cardinalities, highlighting the limitations of first-order logic in controlling the sizes of its models.

## o686 entry: LP

LP
See Logic of Paradox.

## o687 section: M

## o688 entry: main connective

main connective
See dominant connective.

## o689 entry: main operator

main operator
See dominant connective.

## o690 entry: major connective

major connective
See dominant connective.

## o691 citation: MakridisLogic

## o692 entry: major premise

major premise
The premise in a syllogism containing the major term, which is the predicate of the conclusion.

## o693 entry: major term

major term
The term that appears as the predicate in the conclusion of a syllogism.

## o694 entry: many-sorted logic

many-sorted logic
A variant of first-order logic that allows for multiple domains of discourse, with variables and quantifiers distinguished by the sort or type of objects they range over.

## o695 entry: many-valued logic

many-valued logic
A logical system that extends beyond classical two-valued true/false logic to include additional truth values, accommodating indeterminacy, uncertainty, or levels of truth.

## o696 entry: markov's principle

markov's principle
A principle in constructive mathematics stating that if it is impossible for a mathematical object not to have a certain property, then there exists an object with that property.

## o697 entry: material biconditional

material biconditional
A logical connective that is true if and only if both operands have the same truth value, also known as the "if and only if" operator.

## o698 entry: material conditional

material conditional
A logical connective typically represented by "if...then...", which is true unless the antecedent is true and the consequent is false.

## o699 entry: material consequence

material consequence
The relationship between statements where the truth of one (the antecedent) results in the truth of another (the consequent), based on the content of the statements rather than their logical form. Contrast formal consequence. See also semantic consequence. Not to be confused with material implication.

## o700 entry: material equivalence

material equivalence
The relation between propositions that hold the same truth value; their truth or falsity under all possible conditions is identical.

## o701 entry: material implication

material implication
Another term for material conditional, emphasizing the implication based on the substantive content of the propositions involved.

## o702 entry: materially equivalent

materially equivalent
Describing propositions that are true under exactly the same conditions or have the same truth value across all possible worlds.

## o703 entry: mathematical abstractionism

mathematical abstractionism
A philosophical stance that views mathematical entities as abstractions from physical objects or properties, rather than as inherently existing objects.

## o704 citation

## o705 entry: mathematical induction

mathematical induction
A method of proof used in mathematics to prove statements about natural numbers, based on proving a base case and an inductive step.

## o706 entry: mathematical induction schema

mathematical induction schema
Synonym of mathematical induction.

## o707 citation

## o708 citation

## o709 entry: mathematical logic

mathematical logic
The study of logic within the framework of mathematical reasoning, focusing on the mathematical properties of logical systems.

## o710 entry: matrix

matrix
The quantifier-free part of a formula in prenex normal form.

## o711 citation

## o712 citation

## o713 entry: maximal consistent set

maximal consistent set
A set of formulas in a logical system that is consistent (no contradictions can be derived from it) and maximal (no additional formulas can be added without causing inconsistency).

## o714 entry: Meinongianism

Meinongianism
A philosophical theory that posits the existence of non-existent objects, named after Alexius Meinong, challenging traditional ontological categories.

## o715 citation: SEP_Nonexistent

## o716 entry: mention

mention
In philosophy, especially in discussions of language, "mention" involves referencing a word or phrase itself rather than employing it for its semantic content. This typically occurs when discussing the word as a linguistic entity. In the use-mention distinction, "mention" is signified by the use of quotation marks or other indicators that the words are subjects of discussion rather than tools for communication. For example, in the sentence "The word 'books' consists of five letters," "books" is mentioned, not use.

## o717 entry: mere possibilia

mere possibilia
Hypothetical or possible entities that do not actually exist but could exist under different circumstances.

## o718 entry: metalanguage

metalanguage
A language used to describe, discuss, or analyze another language (the object language), especially in the context of logic and linguistics.

## o719 entry: metalogic

metalogic
The study of the properties of logical systems and languages, including their consistency, completeness, and decidability.

## o720 entry: metamathematics

metamathematics
The branch of mathematics that studies mathematical systems and theories from a higher-level perspective, often using methods from mathematical logic.

## o721 entry: metatheorem

metatheorem
A theorem about a mathematical theory or logical system, stating properties or results about the system itself.

## o722 entry: metatheory

metatheory
The theoretical analysis of mathematical theories or logical systems, focusing on their properties, structure, and foundations.

## o723 entry: middle term

middle term
In a syllogism, the term that appears in both premises but not in the conclusion, serving as a link between the major and minor terms.

## o724 entry: minimalism

minimalism
A deflationism view of truth, according to which all instances of Tarski's T-schema are trivially (or analytically) true.

## o725 citation

## o726 entry: minimization

minimization
In recursive function theory, the operation of searching for the smallest witness to a decidable predicate.

## o727 entry: minor premise

minor premise
The premise in a syllogism that includes the minor term, which is the subject of the conclusion.

## o728 entry: minor term

minor term
The term that appears as the subject in the conclusion of a syllogism.

## o729 entry: modal actualism

modal actualism
The philosophical position that only actual, existing objects are possible, denying the existence of merely possible objects.

## o730 entry: modal agnosticism

modal agnosticism
A stance of neutrality or skepticism regarding the truth of modal propositions, especially concerning necessity and possibility.

## o731 citation

## o732 citation

## o733 entry: modal fictionalism

modal fictionalism
The view that modal statements (about possibility and necessity) can be treated as useful fictions without committing to the existence of possible worlds.

## o734 entry: modal logic

modal logic
A branch of logic that deals with modalities such as necessity, possibility, and related concepts, often formalized through the use of modal operators.

## o735 entry: modal operator

modal operator
A logical operator that modifies the truth value of a statement by expressing modality, such as necessity ($\Box$) or possibility ($\Diamond$).

## o736 entry: modal possibilism

modal possibilism
The view that possible but non-actual entities exist in some sense or in possible worlds, in contrast to actualism.

## o737 citation: SEP_Actualism

## o738 entry: modal realism

modal realism
The philosophical position that possible worlds are as real as the actual world, and that individuals can exist across different possible worlds.

## o739 entry: modality

modality
A quality of propositions that expresses mode or manner, particularly in terms of necessity, possibility, and other related concepts.

## o740 entry: model

model
In logic and mathematics, a structure that interprets the symbols of a language and satisfies the statements of a theory or system.

## o741 entry: model-theoretic consequence

model-theoretic consequence
See semantic consequence.

## o742 citation

## o743 citation

## o744 citation

## o745 entry: model-theoretic validity

model-theoretic validity
See semantic validity.

## o746 entry: model theory

model theory
The branch of mathematical logic that studies the relationship between formal languages and their interpretations or models.

## o747 entry: modus ponens

modus ponens
A rule of inference that allows one to derive a conclusion from a conditional statement and its antecedent, formalized as if $P \rightarrow Q$ and $P$, then $Q$.

## o748 entry: modus tollens

modus tollens
A rule of inference that allows one to derive a conclusion from a conditional statement and the negation of its consequent, formalized as if $P \rightarrow Q$ and $\neg Q$, then $\neg P$.

## o749 entry: molecule

molecule
In logic and philosophy, often used metaphorically to refer to a compound entity or concept that is made up of simpler, atomic parts.

## o750 citation: SCKleene_MathLogic

## o751 entry: monadic first-order logic

monadic first-order logic
A variant of first-order logic restricted to predicates that take only one argument, focusing on properties of individual objects rather than relations between them.

## o752 entry: monadic function

monadic function
See unary function.

## o753 entry: monadic predicate

monadic predicate
A predicate that takes a single argument, used to express properties of objects or entities within a domain of discourse.

## o754 citation

## o755 entry: monadic predicate logic

monadic predicate logic
First-order logic with predicates that only take one argument, allowing for the expression of properties of individuals.

## o756 entry: monadic relation

monadic relation
See unary relation.

## o757 entry: monism

monism
See logical monism.

## o758 entry: monomorphism

monomorphism
A morphism in category theory that is left-cancellable, meaning if two compositions with it are equal, then the other morphisms must be equal, akin to an injective function in set theory.

## o759 entry: monotonic logic

monotonic logic
A type of logic in which adding new premises to a set does not decrease the set of conclusions that can be derived, ensuring that conclusions are preserved under the addition of new information.

## o760 entry: monotonicity

monotonicity
The property of a function or process that preserves order, in logic, referring to systems where conclusions derived from a set of premises are not invalidated by adding more premises.

## o761 entry: mood

mood
See syllogistic mood.

## o762 entry: multi-modal logic

multi-modal logic
A system of modal logic that incorporates more than one kind of modality, such as necessity, possibility, knowledge, and belief.

## o763 entry: multivalent logic

multivalent logic
Another term for many-valued logic, emphasizing the presence of more than two truth values beyond the classical true and false.

## o764 entry: mutually exclusive

mutually exclusive
Referring to events or propositions that cannot both occur or be true at the same time, indicating a logical or statistical relationship of exclusion.

## o765 section: N

## o766 entry: nand

nand
A logical operation meaning "not and"; it produces a true result for all input combinations except the case where all inputs are true. It is a fundamental operation since any logical function can be constructed using only NAND operations.

## o767 entry: n-ary function

n-ary function
A function that takes n arguments, where n is a natural number, generalizing the concept of binary functions to functions of any arity.

## o768 entry: n-ary relation

n-ary relation
A relation that involves n elements, where n is a natural number, extending the concept of binary relations to relations between more than two entities.

## o769 entry: natural deduction

natural deduction
A system of logical inference that attempts to mirror the intuitive ways humans reason, consisting of a set of inference rules for introducing and eliminating logical connectives.

## o770 entry: natural language

natural language
A language that has developed naturally in human societies as a means of communication, as opposed to artificial or constructed languages.

## o771 entry: natural numbers

natural numbers
The set of positive integers, often denoted by $\mathbb{N}$, and typically including zero. Used as the basis for counting and ordering.

## o772 entry: necessary condition

necessary condition
A condition that must be satisfied for a statement to be true but is not sufficient on its own to guarantee the statement's truth.

## o773 entry: necessitation rule

necessitation rule
In modal logic, a rule stating that if a proposition is a theorem, then its necessity is also a theorem.

## o774 entry: necessity

necessity
A modal quality indicating that a proposition is true in all possible worlds or under all possible circumstances.

## o775 entry: necessity of identity

necessity of identity
The principle stating that if two objects are identical, they are necessarily identical in all possible worlds.

## o776 entry: negation

negation
A logical operation that inverts the truth value of a proposition; it turns a true statement into a false one and vice versa.

## o777 entry: negation completeness

negation completeness
The property of a logical system where every statement or its negation is provable within the system.

## o778 citation

## o779 citation

## o780 entry: negation consistency

negation consistency
The consistency of a logical system in which no statement is both provable and disprovable, including its negation.

## o781 citation

## o782 citation

## o783 citation

## o784 entry: negation elimination

negation elimination
A rule in natural deduction that allows the derivation of a conclusion by eliminating a negation, under certain conditions.

## o785 entry: negation introduction

negation introduction
A rule in natural deduction that allows for the introduction of negation into a proof, typically by deriving a contradiction from the assumption that the negation is false.

## o786 entry: negation normal form

negation normal form
A way of expressing logical formulas where negation is only applied directly to atomic propositions, and the only other allowed connectives are conjunction and disjunction.

## o787 entry: negative proposition

negative proposition
A proposition that asserts the non-existence or absence of something, or denies some property of an object.

## o788 citation

## o789 citation

## o790 entry: neo-Fregeanism

neo-Fregeanism
A philosophical stance revisiting Frege's logicism with the aim of grounding mathematics, particularly arithmetic and analysis, in logic through the use of Hume's Principle and other axioms.

## o791 entry: neo-logicism

neo-logicism
A movement in the philosophy of mathematics seeking to revive logicism, the project of founding mathematics on logic, through new insights and approaches, particularly in the wake of criticisms of traditional logicism.

## o792 entry: New Foundations

New Foundations
A system of set theory proposed by W.V. Quine with a distinctive axiom schema intended to avoid the paradoxes of naïve set theory while allowing a universal set.

## o793 entry: nominalism

nominalism
The philosophical view that abstract concepts, general terms, or universals have no independent existence but exist only as names or labels for groups of individual objects.

## o794 entry: non-alethic modal logic

non-alethic modal logic
A form of modal logic that deals with modes of truth beyond the alethic modes of necessity and possibility, such as deontic (duty and permission) or epistemic (knowledge and belief) modalities.

## o795 citation

## o796 citation

## o797 entry: non-classical logic

non-classical logic
Any logical system that diverges from the principles of classical logic, including intuitionistic logic, many-valued logics, modal logics, and others that challenge classical assumptions or introduce new principles.

## o798 entry: non-commutative logic

non-commutative logic
A logical system in which the order of application of operations affects the outcome, contrasting with classical logic where operations like conjunction and disjunction are commutative.

## o799 entry: non-deterministic polynomial time

non-deterministic polynomial time
The complexity class NP, consisting of decision problems for which a 'yes' answer can be verified by a deterministic Turing machine in polynomial time, given the correct certificate or witness.

## o800 entry: non-deterministic turing machine

non-deterministic turing machine
A theoretical model of computation that, at each step, can make a 'choice' from multiple possibilities, allowing it to explore many possible branches of execution simultaneously.

## o801 entry: non-standard logic

non-standard logic
Logics that diverge from or extend classical logic, including non-classical logics, many-valued logics, and modal logics, among others. Also called non-classical logic.

## o802 citation

## o803 citation

## o804 entry: non-standard model

non-standard model
A model of a theory that satisfies the axioms of the theory but has properties not intended by the original formulation, often revealing the theory's consistency or independence results.

## o805 entry: normal form

normal form
In logic, a standardized way of expressing logical formulas, such as conjunctive normal form (CNF) or disjunctive normal form (DNF), to facilitate analysis or computation.

## o806 entry: normal modal logic

normal modal logic
A class of modal logics that include the necessitation rule and the distribution axiom, allowing for the derivation of necessary truths from given axioms and rules of inference.

## o807 entry: NP

NP
A complexity class (nondeterministic polynomial time) that includes decision problems for which a 'yes' answer can be verified in polynomial time by a deterministic Turing machine.

## o808 entry: NP-complete

NP-complete
A class of decision problems in NP for which any problem in NP can be reduced to it in polynomial time, and whose solution can be verified in polynomial time; considered among the hardest problems in NP.

## o809 entry: numerical quantifier

numerical quantifier
A quantifier that specifies the exact number of instances for which a predicate holds within a domain of discourse, such as 'exactly three', 'at least five'.

## o810 citation

## o811 citation

## o812 citation

## o813 section: O

## o814 entry: object language

object language
The language that is being studied, analyzed, or used to construct statements in a logical system, as opposed to the metalanguage used to talk about the object language.

## o815 citation: SEP_TarskiTruth

## o816 citation

## o817 entry: objectual quantifier

objectual quantifier
A type of quantifier in formal logic that quantifies over objects in the domain of discourse, as opposed to substituting variables with constants or other expressions.

## o818 citation

## o819 citation

## o820 entry: obverse

obverse
In traditional logic, the process of negating the predicate of a categorical proposition and changing its quality (affirmative to negative, or vice versa) without altering its truth value.

## o821 entry: obversion

obversion
The operation of forming the obverse of a categorical proposition, resulting in an equivalent statement with a negated predicate and opposite quality.

## o822 entry: one-to-one

one-to-one
A function or mapping from one set to another where each element of the first set is associated with a unique element of the second set; also known as injective.

## o823 entry: ontic vagueness

ontic vagueness
Vagueness inherent in the nature of reality itself, as opposed to semantic vagueness or epistemic vagueness which relate to language or knowledge, respectively. See also in rebus vagueness.

## o824 citation

## o825 citation

## o826 entry: onto

onto
A function or mapping from one set to another where every element of the second set is associated with at least one element of the first set; also known as surjective.

## o827 entry: open formula

open formula
A formula in a formal language that contains free variables, meaning it cannot be determined as true or false until the variables are bound or specified.

## o828 entry: open pair

open pair
A paradox about a pair of statements that deny each other.

## o829 citation

## o830 citation

## o831 entry: open term

open term
An expression in a formal language that contains free variables, which does not denote a specific object or truth value until the variables are instantiated.

## o832 entry: o-proposition

o-proposition
In traditional logic, a particular negative categorical proposition, stating that some members of the subject class are not members of the predicate class.

## o833 citation

## o834 entry: or

or
A logical connective (disjunction) that links propositions in a way that the compound proposition is true if at least one of the linked propositions is true.

## o835 entry: ordered logic

ordered logic
A logic without weakening and permutation.

## o836 citation

## o837 citation

## o838 entry: ordered n-tuple

ordered n-tuple
A sequence of elements in which the order of the elements matters, generalizing the concept of an ordered pair to sequences of any length.

## o839 entry: ordered pair

ordered pair
A fundamental structure in mathematics and logic that consists of two elements arranged in a specific order, typically represented as (a, b).

## o840 entry: ostensive definition

ostensive definition
A definition that explains the meaning of a term or symbol by pointing to examples and counterexamples of the concept it represents.

## o841 section: P

## o842 entry: pairing function

pairing function
A function that uniquely associates pairs of numbers with a single number, allowing pairs to be encoded as single values.

## o843 entry: paraconsistent logic

paraconsistent logic
A non-classical logic that allows for contradictions to exist without deriving absurdity, useful in modeling inconsistent but non-trivial systems.

## o844 entry: paradox

paradox
A statement or situation that contradicts itself or defies intuition, often challenging existing understandings of logic and truth.

## o845 entry: paradoxes of material implication

paradoxes of material implication
Paradoxes that arise from the counterintuitive consequences of the material conditional, especially when the antecedent is false or when there is no causal or necessary connection between the antecedent and consequent.

## o846 entry: parameter

parameter
A parameter is an expression whose referent is assumed to be fixed relative to a particular situation, but whose value can vary across situations.

## o847 entry: partial logic

partial logic
A logical system that allows for statements that are neither true nor false, accommodating undefined terms or indeterminate truth values.

## o848 citation

## o849 citation

## o850 entry: particular proposition

particular proposition
In traditional logic, a proposition that asserts something about some members of a class, as opposed to all members.

## o851 entry: Peano arithmetic

Peano arithmetic
A formal system for arithmetic based on axioms proposed by Giuseppe Peano, foundational for the theory of natural numbers.

## o852 entry: Peirce's law

Peirce's law
A principle in logic (($P \rightarrow Q) \rightarrow P) \rightarrow P$ that is valid in classical but not in intuitionistic logic, named after Charles Sanders Peirce.

## o853 entry: permutation

permutation
The structural rule that allows one to exchange two formulas that are on the same side of the arrow.

## o854 citation

## o855 citation

## o856 entry: permutation invariant

permutation invariant
A property of a function or relation that remains unchanged when the elements of its arguments are permuted.

## o857 citation

## o858 citation

## o859 entry: persuasive definition

persuasive definition
A definition intended to influence attitudes or stir emotions by embedding an evaluative component, often used in ethical or political arguments.

## o860 entry: petitio principii

petitio principii
Also known as begging the question, an informal fallacy where the conclusion of an argument is assumed in one of the premises.

## o861 entry: Philonian conditional

Philonian conditional
Another term for the material conditional, emphasizing its use in propositional logic to represent "if...then..." statements without implying a causal relation.

## o862 citation

## o863 entry: philosophical logic

philosophical logic
The study of the more abstract or theoretical aspects of logic, often concerning questions about reference, modality, quantification, and the structure of propositions and arguments.

## o864 entry: philosophy of logic

philosophy of logic
A branch of philosophy that examines the nature and scope of logic, including the assumptions, methodologies, and implications of various logical systems.

## o865 entry: platonism

platonism
In the philosophy of mathematics, the view that abstract mathematical objects exist independently of human thought.

## o866 entry: Plato's beard

Plato's beard
A metaphorical expression attributed to Willard Van Orman Quine, referring to the problem of non-existent entities and their properties.

## o867 entry: plural quantification

plural quantification
Quantification over multiple objects or entities considered together, extending beyond singular quantification to express statements about sets or groups.

## o868 entry: pluralism

pluralism
See logical pluralism.

## o869 entry: Polish notation

Polish notation
A prefix notation for logic and arithmetic where operators precede their operands, eliminating the need for parentheses to indicate order of operation.

## o870 entry: polyadic first-order logic

polyadic first-order logic
First-order logic extended to include predicates with more than one argument, allowing for the expression of relations between multiple objects.

## o871 citation

## o872 citation

## o873 entry: possibility

possibility
A modality indicating that a proposition may be true, even if it is not actually true; the capacity for some state of affairs to occur.

## o874 citation

## o875 citation

## o876 entry: possible world

possible world
A hypothetical total way things might have been or could be, used in modal logic to analyze possibility, necessity, and other modal concepts.

## o877 entry: Post consistency

Post consistency
A theory is "Post consistent" (or absolutely consistent) if and only if there is at least one statement in the language of the theory that is not a theorem; otherwise, it is "Post inconsistent".

## o878 citation

## o879 citation

## o880 entry: post hoc, ergo propter hoc

post hoc, ergo propter hoc
A logical fallacy that assumes that if one event occurs after another, then the first event must be the cause of the second.

## o881 entry: postulate

postulate
A proposition accepted as true to provide a logical basis.

## o882 entry: pragmatics

pragmatics
The study of how context influences the interpretation of meaning in language, beyond the literal meaning of words or the structure of sentences.

## o883 entry: predicate

predicate
A function or relation that asserts a property about or a relationship between individuals or objects in a domain of discourse.

## o884 entry: predicate functor

predicate functor
In logic, a symbol that represents a function from individuals or tuples of individuals to truth values, essentially a generalization of a predicate.

## o885 citation

## o886 entry: predicate functor logic

predicate functor logic
A logical system that combines elements of predicate logic with the concept of functors, allowing for a more expressive representation of properties and relations.

## o887 entry: predicate term

predicate term
The second term of a proposition in a syllogism.

## o888 citation

## o889 citation

## o890 entry: prefix notation

prefix notation
A way of writing mathematical and logical expressions where the operator precedes its operands, facilitating unambiguous interpretation without parentheses.

## o891 entry: prelinearity axiom

prelinearity axiom
The formula (P → Q) ∨ (Q → P).

## o892 citation

## o893 citation

## o894 entry: premise

premise
A statement in an argument that provides support or evidence for the conclusion.

## o895 entry: prenex normal form

prenex normal form
A form of logical expression where all quantifiers are moved to the front, standardizing the structure of first-order logical statements.

## o896 entry: primitive recursion

primitive recursion
A form of recursion where a function is defined in terms of itself, using simpler cases, with a base case to stop the recursion.

## o897 entry: primitive recursive function

primitive recursive function
A function computable by a primitive recursive algorithm, representing a class of functions that can be defined by initial functions and operations of composition and primitive recursion.

## o898 citation: SEP_recursive

## o899 entry: primitive recursive relation

primitive recursive relation
A relation that can be defined by primitive recursive functions, characterizing a subset of computable relations.

## o900 entry: principal connective

principal connective
See dominant connective.

## o901 entry: probability calculus

probability calculus
The branch of mathematics dealing with probability, including the laws and formulas that govern random variables and events.

## o902 entry: probability logic

probability logic
A logical system that incorporates probabilistic elements to deal with uncertainty, extending classical logic to handle degrees of belief or likelihood.

## o903 entry: probability theory

probability theory
The mathematical study of randomness and uncertainty, focusing on the analysis of random variables, events, and processes.

## o904 entry: proof

proof
A logical or mathematical argument that demonstrates the truth of a statement or theorem, based on axioms, definitions, and previously established theorems.

## o905 entry: proof by cases

proof by cases
A proof technique that divides the proof into several cases, showing that the statement to be proved holds in each case.

## o906 entry: proof by induction

proof by induction
A method of mathematical proof used to establish the truth of an infinite number of cases, based on a base case and an inductive step.

## o907 entry: proof theory

proof theory
The branch of mathematical logic that studies the structure and properties of mathematical proofs, aiming to understand and formalize the process of mathematical reasoning.

## o908 entry: proof-theoretic consequence

proof-theoretic consequence
See syntactic consequence.

## o909 citation

## o910 citation

## o911 entry: proof-theoretic semantics

proof-theoretic semantics
An alternative to truth-condition semantics (also known as model-theoretic semantics), focusing on proof rather than on truth.

## o912 entry: proof-theoretic validity

proof-theoretic validity
Referring to a valid proof in proof-theoretic semantics.

## o913 entry: property

property
See concept.

## o914 entry: proposition

proposition
A declarative statement that is capable of being true or false, serving as the basic unit of meaning in logic and philosophy.

## o915 entry: propositional attitude

propositional attitude
A mental state expressed by verbs such as believe, desire, hope, and know, followed by a proposition, reflecting an individual's attitude towards the truth of the proposition.

## o916 entry: propositional connective

propositional connective
See logical connective.

## o917 entry: propositional function

propositional function
An expression that becomes a proposition when values are assigned to its variables, essentially a predicate that can take one or more arguments.

## o918 entry: propositional letter

propositional letter
A variable in propositional logic that represents a specific, undefined proposition, used as a placeholder in logical formulas.

## o919 entry: propositional logic

propositional logic
The branch of logic that deals with propositions as units and uses propositional connectives to construct complex statements, focusing on the truth-values of propositions.

## o920 entry: prosentential

prosentential
Pertaining to the theory that some expressions function as whole propositions rather than referring to objects or describing properties.

## o921 entry: prosentential theory of truth

prosentential theory of truth
A theory that regards truth as a prosentential operator, simplifying the analysis of truth statements without invoking propositions as entities.

## o922 citation

## o923 entry: provability logic

provability logic
A branch of modal logic concerned with the properties of provability and modalities that express notions of necessity as provability within a formal system.

## o924 entry: provability predicate

provability predicate
A predicate, often called "Bew", that expresses the concept of a statement being provable within a given formal system.

## o925 citation

## o926 entry: pseudo modus ponens

pseudo modus ponens
Synonym for assertion, the axiom that (A ∧ (A → B)) → B.

## o927 citation

## o928 citation

## o929 entry: punctuation

punctuation
In logic, refers to parentheses and brackets.

## o930 citation: beallbasics2ed

## o931 entry: pure first-order logic

pure first-order logic
The system of first-order logic that contains no function symbols or identity, only predicate symbols.

## o932 citation

## o933 entry: pure predicate logic

pure predicate logic
See pure first-order logic.

## o934 entry: Putnam's model-theoretic argument

Putnam's model-theoretic argument
An argument by Hilary Putnam challenging the conventional understanding of reference and truth, suggesting that semantic externalism leads to radical skepticism about the meanings of terms and the contents of thoughts.

## o935 citation

## o936 section: Q

## o937 entry: quality

quality
Refers to whether a categorical proposition is affirmative or negative.

## o938 entry: quantified modal logic

quantified modal logic
An extension of modal logic that includes quantifiers such as "all" and "some", allowing for expressions involving necessity or possibility applied to individuals or properties quantitatively.

## o939 citation

## o940 entry: quantifier

quantifier
A logical operator that specifies the quantity of specimens in the domain of discourse that satisfy an open formula, such as "all", "some", or "exists".

## o941 entry: quantifier shift fallacy

quantifier shift fallacy
A logical fallacy involving the incorrect interchange of the position of two quantifiers, or a quantifier and a modal operator, leading to invalid conclusions.

## o942 entry: quantity

quantity
Refers to a categorical proposition's being universal or particular.

## o943 citation

## o944 entry: quantum logic

quantum logic
A non-classical logic that attempts to capture the peculiarities of quantum mechanics, challenging traditional logical principles such as the law of excluded middle and distributivity.

## o945 entry: Quine's dictum

Quine's dictum
The principle that "To be is to be the value of a variable", emphasizing ontological commitment in terms of quantification and the variables of quantified theory.

## o946 citation

## o947 citation

## o948 citation

## o949 entry: quod erat demonstrandum (QED)

quod erat demonstrandum (QED)
A Latin phrase meaning "which was to be demonstrated", traditionally used at the end of a mathematical proof or logical argument to signify its completion.

## o950 entry: quotation

quotation
The practice of repeating words or text from another source, often indicated in writing by quotation marks. In logic, it involves referring to the expression itself rather than its meaning.

## o951 section: R

## o952 entry: ramified theory of types

ramified theory of types
An extension of the simple theory of types that includes a hierarchy of levels, allowing for the distinction between objects and functions at different orders to avoid paradoxes such as Russell's paradox.

## o953 entry: ramsey sentence

ramsey sentence
A way of expressing the empirical content of a theory by replacing its theoretical terms with existential quantifiers, named after Frank P. Ramsey.

## o954 entry: Ramsey test

Ramsey test
A criterion for evaluating the acceptability of conditional statements in terms of belief revision: if adding the antecedent to one's stock of beliefs requires adding the consequent for consistency, then the conditional is accepted.

## o955 entry: range

range
The set of all possible outputs or values that a function can produce, given all possible inputs.

## o956 entry: recursion

recursion
The process of defining something in terms of itself, often used in mathematics and computer science to define functions, sequences, and structures.

## o957 entry: recursion theorem

recursion theorem
Master theorem (analysis of algorithms)
Kleene's recursion theorem

## o958 entry: recursive definition

recursive definition
A definition of a function, set, or other mathematical object that is defined in terms of itself, using a base case and a rule for generating subsequent elements.

## o959 entry: recursive function

recursive function
A function that can be computed by a procedure that calls itself, directly or indirectly, with a base case to prevent infinite recursion.

## o960 entry: recursive function theory

recursive function theory
The study of recursive functions and their properties, including their computability and classification into hierarchies of complexity.

## o961 entry: recursive relation

recursive relation
A relation defined on a set where the relation is specified in terms of itself, allowing for the construction of complex relational structures from simpler ones.

## o962 entry: recursively axiomatizable theory

recursively axiomatizable theory
A theory for which there exists a recursive set of axioms that can generate all theorems of the theory through logical deduction.

## o963 citation

## o964 citation

## o965 citation

## o966 entry: red herring

red herring
An informal fallacy or rhetorical strategy that diverts attention from the original issue or argument by introducing an irrelevant topic.

## o967 entry: reductio ad absurdum

reductio ad absurdum
A method of argument that demonstrates the falsity of a statement by showing that it logically leads to a contradiction or absurd conclusion.

## o968 entry: redundancy theory of truth

redundancy theory of truth
A theory of truth that suggests stating that a proposition is true is redundant and does not add anything to the proposition's content.

## o969 entry: reference

reference
The relation between words, phrases, or symbols and the objects, concepts, or events they denote or stand for.

## o970 citation: RoutledgeSenseReference

## o971 entry: referential opacity

referential opacity
A property of expressions wherein substituting a co-referential term does not necessarily preserve truth, typically occurring in intensional contexts like belief reports.

## o972 entry: reflexivity

reflexivity
The property of a relation where every element is related to itself, such as the equality relation.

## o973 entry: refutation

refutation
The process of disproving or showing the falseness of a statement, argument, or theory.

## o974 entry: register computable

register computable
Computable by a register machine.

## o975 citation

## o976 citation

## o977 entry: register machine

register machine
A theoretical model of computation that uses a set of registers to store numbers and a program of instructions to perform calculations, serving as an alternative to the Turing machine model.

## o978 entry: relational semantics

relational semantics
An approach to interpreting logical languages where the meaning of sentences is defined in terms of relations between possible worlds or states of affairs, commonly used in modal and temporal logics.

## o979 entry: relative consistency proof

relative consistency proof
A proof showing that if a mathematical system $S$ is consistent, then an extension of $S$ by adding new axioms is also consistent, used to compare the foundational strength of different theories.

## o980 entry: relevance logic

relevance logic
A non-classical logic that seeks to capture the notion that the premises of a valid argument must be relevant to the conclusion, avoiding paradoxes of material implication.

## o981 entry: representation

representation
A n+1-ary predicate P represents an n-ary function f if, and only if, it is the case that: $Px_1, x_2, \ldots x_n, y$ is true if, and only if, $f(x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_n) = y$. Similarly, a unary predicate P represents a set S if, and only if, it is the case that: Px is true if, and only if, x is a member of S.

## o982 entry: reverse mathematics

reverse mathematics
A program in mathematical logic that seeks to determine which axioms are necessary to prove theorems of mathematics by proving theorems from the weakest possible systems.

## o983 entry: revision theory of truth

revision theory of truth
A theory proposing a non-classical approach to the concept of truth, suggesting that truth values of propositions can be revised in light of paradoxes, notably the liar paradox.

## o984 citation

## o985 entry: rigid designator

rigid designator
A term that refers to the same object in all possible worlds where that object exists, used in discussions of necessity and identity across possible worlds.

## o986 entry: Robinson arithmetic

Robinson arithmetic
A fragment of Peano arithmetic that omits the axiom schema of induction, serving as a foundation for arithmetic that is weaker but still expressive.

## o987 entry: Ross paradox

Ross paradox
A paradox in deontic logic arising from imperatives that imply counterintuitive obligations, demonstrating challenges in formalizing moral and ethical reasoning.

## o988 entry: rule of inference

rule of inference
A logical rule that justifies the transition from a set of premises to a conclusion, forming the basis of deductive reasoning.

## o989 entry: rule of replacement

rule of replacement
A rule in formal logic allowing for the substitution of equivalent expressions within logical proofs, maintaining the validity of the argument.

## o990 section: S

## o991 entry: salve veritate

salve veritate
A Latin phrase meaning "with truth unchanged", used in logic and philosophy to indicate a transformation or substitution that preserves the truth value of statements.

## o992 citation

## o993 entry: satisfaction

satisfaction
In model theory, the relation between a structure and a sentence where the structure makes the sentence true, according to the interpretation of the sentence's symbols in that structure.

## o994 citation

## o995 entry: satisfiability

satisfiability
The property of a logical formula if there exists at least one interpretation under which the formula is true.

## o996 entry: schema

schema
A template or pattern representing a class of similar statements or propositions, often used in the formulation of logical axioms and rules.

## o997 citation

## o998 citation

## o999 citation

## o1000 entry: scope

scope
The part of a logical formula to which an operator, quantifier, or modifier applies, determining the extent of its operation.

## o1001 entry: Scott

Scott
Dana Scott

## o1002 entry: sea battle

sea battle
See Aristotle's sea battle.

## o1003 entry: second-order logic

second-order logic
A logic that extends first-order logic by allowing quantification over predicates and relations, in addition to quantification over individuals.

## o1004 entry: self-contradictory

self-contradictory
A property of a statement or proposition that contradicts itself, making it impossible to be true.

## o1005 entry: self-reference

self-reference
The act of referring to oneself or itself, used in discussions of paradoxes, reflexivity, and the foundations of mathematics and logic.

## o1006 entry: semantic conception of truth

semantic conception of truth
A theory of truth based on the correspondence between statements and facts or states of affairs in the world, emphasizing the role of meaning and interpretation.

## o1007 citation

## o1008 entry: semantic consequence

semantic consequence
The relation that holds between a set of premises and a conclusion formulated within a certain formal language, such that, for this language, there is no possible interpretation that evaluates the premises as true and the conclusion as false.

## o1009 citation

## o1010 citation

## o1011 citation

## o1012 entry: semantic paradox

semantic paradox
A paradox that arises due to some peculiarity of semantic concepts, such as truth, falsity, and definability, as distinguished from logical or set-theoretical concepts. The distinction between semantical and logical paradoxes is controversial and was invented by Ramsey.

## o1013 citation: BunninYu

## o1014 citation

## o1015 entry: semantic tableau

semantic tableau
A method of proof in logic that uses a tree structure to systematically explore the truth or falsity of logical expressions by breaking them down into simpler components.

## o1016 entry: semantic vagueness

semantic vagueness
The view that vagueness is a feature of language, rather than of the world or of human knowledge. Contrast in rebus vagueness and epistemic vagueness.

## o1017 entry: semantic value

semantic value
The meaning or content assigned to a linguistic expression in semantics, including truth values for sentences in logical languages.

## o1018 citation

## o1019 citation

## o1020 entry: semantic validity

semantic validity
A formula is called semantically valid, or a tautology, if it is true under every interpretation.

## o1021 citation

## o1022 citation

## o1023 citation: chowdharyAI

## o1024 entry: semantically closed language

semantically closed language
A language that contains its own truth predicate, allowing statements about the truth of sentences formulated within the same language.

## o1025 citation: PhilosopyOfLogicElsevier

## o1026 citation

## o1027 entry: semantics

semantics
The study of meaning in language, including the interpretation of words, phrases, sentences, and texts, and the study of the principles that govern the assignment of meanings.

## o1028 entry: semi-decidable theory

semi-decidable theory
A theory for which there exists an algorithm that can enumerate all its theorems, but there may not be an algorithm to decide non-theorems.

## o1029 citation

## o1030 entry: sense

sense
The aspect of meaning that pertains to the inherent content or connotation of an expression, distinct from its reference or denotation.

## o1031 entry: sensible formula

sensible formula
A formula with no vacuous quantifiers.

## o1032 citation: BostockIntermediate

## o1033 citation

## o1034 entry: sentence letter

sentence letter
A symbol used in propositional logic to represent an arbitrary proposition, serving as a placeholder in logical formulas.

## o1035 entry: sentential logic

sentential logic
Another term for propositional logic, focusing on the logical relationships between whole sentences or propositions rather than their internal structure.

## o1036 entry: sequence

sequence
An ordered list of objects or terms, each of which is identified by its position in the list, used in mathematics and logic to define functions, sets, and series.

## o1037 entry: sequent

sequent
In sequent calculus, a formal representation of a logical deduction, consisting of a sequence of formulas that precede a turnstile and a sequence of formulas that follow it, indicating premises and conclusion.

## o1038 entry: sequent calculus

sequent calculus
A formal system for deriving logical entailments, representing deductions as sequences of formulas, and emphasizing the structural rules of logical derivation.

## o1039 entry: seriality

seriality
The property of a relation where for every element in the domain, there exists an element in the codomain that is related to another element in a specific way, particularly in the context of binary relations and modal logic.

## o1040 entry: S4

S4
A modal logic system characterized by the axioms that necessitate reflexivity and transitivity for the accessibility relation, implying that if something is necessary, then it is necessarily necessary.

## o1041 entry: sharpening

sharpening
The process of making a vague or imprecise concept more precise or clearly defined, often discussed in the context of semantic vagueness.

## o1042 citation

## o1043 citation

## o1044 entry: Sheffer stroke

Sheffer stroke
A logical operation equivalent to the nand (not and) function; it is functionally complete, meaning all other logical operations can be constructed from it.

## o1045 entry: signature

signature
In logic and algebra, a set of symbols along with their arities, defining the kinds of operations, functions, and relations considered in a structure or theory.

## o1046 citation

## o1047 entry: simple type theory

simple type theory
A type theory that divides objects into a simple hierarchy of objects, classes of objects, classes of classes of objects, etc. The adjective "simple" is used to contrast it with ramified type theory, which further stratifies these simple types into orders.

## o1048 citation

## o1049 entry: single turnstile

single turnstile
A symbol used in logic ($\vdash$) to denote syntactic entailment, indicating that a formula or set of formulas derives or proves another formula within a formal system.

## o1050 citation

## o1051 entry: singular proposition

singular proposition
A proposition that makes a statement about a specific individual or unique object, as opposed to general statements about classes or categories.

## o1052 entry: singular term

singular term
A term in logic that refers to a single object or entity, distinguishing it from general terms that may refer to classes of objects or properties.

## o1053 entry: situation

situation
In logic and philosophy, a set of circumstances or a state of affairs to which truth-values of statements are relative, often used in situation semantics.

## o1054 citation

## o1055 entry: situation semantics

situation semantics
An approach to semantics that analyzes meaning in terms of situations, rather than attempting to account for meaning solely in terms of truth conditions at possible worlds.

## o1056 entry: skolemization

skolemization
A technique in first-order logic for eliminating existential quantifiers by introducing Skolem functions, used in the process of converting formulas to a standard form.

## o1057 entry: Skolem-Lowenheim theorem

Skolem-Lowenheim theorem
A theorem stating that if a first-order theory has an infinite model, then it has models of every infinite cardinality, highlighting the flexibility of first-order semantics.

## o1058 entry: Skolem normal form

Skolem normal form
A way of expressing first-order logic formulas where all existential quantifiers are moved inside and replaced by Skolem functions, leaving only universal quantifiers at the front.

## o1059 entry: Skolem paradox

Skolem paradox
The apparent paradox arising from the Skolem-Lowenheim theorem, where countable models can be found for theories that intuitively require uncountably many objects, challenging notions of absolute size in set theory.

## o1060 entry: slingshot argument

slingshot argument
An argument aiming to show that all true statements refer to the same "fact" or "entity", raising questions about the correspondence theory of truth and the nature of facts.

## o1061 entry: slippery slope

slippery slope
An informal fallacy or rhetorical argument suggesting that a relatively small first step or minor decision will lead to a chain of related events culminating in a significant (often negative) outcome, without sufficient justification for such inevitability.

## o1062 entry: s-m-n theorem

s-m-n theorem
A fundamental theorem in the theory of computable functions that provides a method for constructing a specific computable function from a given computable function, highlighting the universality and flexibility of computable functions.

## o1063 entry: sophism

sophism
An argument or form of reasoning deemed fallacious, misleading, or deceptive, historically associated with the Sophists in ancient Greece, who were known for their rhetorical skill and relativistic views on truth and morality.

## o1064 citation

## o1065 entry: sophisma

sophisma
A puzzle or paradoxical question that challenges conventional wisdom or logical reasoning, often used in medieval logic to teach students about logical fallacies and the complexities of language.

## o1066 citation

## o1067 entry: sorites paradox

sorites paradox
A paradox arising from vague predicates and the problem of heap, illustrating how a series of seemingly acceptable premises can lead to a paradoxical or absurd conclusion.

## o1068 entry: sorites series

sorites series
A sequence of propositions associated with the sorites paradox, each adding a small amount to the previous one, challenging the boundary between truth and falsehood for vague concepts.

## o1069 entry: sound deductive argument

sound deductive argument
An argument where the logical structure ensures the truth of the conclusion if the premises are true, and where the premises are indeed true, making the argument both valid and sound.

## o1070 entry: soundness

soundness
The property of a logical system wherein every theorem that can be derived using the system's rules is true in every interpretation of the system.

## o1071 entry: square of opposition

square of opposition
A diagram representing the logical relationships between the four types of categorical propositions (A, E, I, O) in traditional logic, showing their contradictions, contraries, subcontraries, and subalternations.

## o1072 entry: statement

statement
An assertion that something is or is not the case, capable of being true or false; the basic unit of discourse in logic.

## o1073 entry: stipulative definition

stipulative definition
A definition that assigns a meaning to a word for the first time, or proposes a new meaning for an existing word, without claiming to capture an already established usage.

## o1074 entry: straw man

straw man
An informal fallacy in which an opponent's argument is overstated or misrepresented in order to be more easily attacked or refuted.

## o1075 entry: strict conditional

strict conditional
A conditional statement interpreted in terms of necessity, such that the truth of the antecedent necessarily implies the truth of the consequent, unlike the material conditional.

## o1076 entry: strict implication

strict implication
A relation between propositions where the truth of the first (the antecedent) necessarily brings about the truth of the second (the consequent), often associated with modal logic.

## o1077 entry: strong completeness

strong completeness
The property of a logical system where if a formula is semantically valid (true in all interpretations), then it is syntactically derivable within the system.

## o1078 citation

## o1079 citation

## o1080 entry: strong counterexample

strong counterexample
Within intuitionistic logic and intuitionistic mathematics, a strong counterexample is a proof of the negation of an instance of some variant of the law of excluded middle.

## o1081 citation

## o1082 citation

## o1083 entry: strong inductive argument

strong inductive argument
An argument that, if the premises are true, provides strong support for the conclusion, making it highly probable but not necessarily true.

## o1084 citation

## o1085 entry: strong Kleene connectives

strong Kleene connectives
Connectives in three-valued logic proposed by Stephen Cole Kleene, designed to deal with indeterminate or undefined values in a logical system.

## o1086 citation

## o1087 entry: strong mathematical induction

strong mathematical induction
A form of mathematical induction that allows one to assume the proposition for all smaller instances simultaneously when proving it for any given instance.

## o1088 entry: strong negation

strong negation
exclusion negation
choice negation

## o1089 entry: strong paraconsistency

strong paraconsistency
Strong paraconsistency is the view that there are possible worlds where contradictions are true, or where some statements are both true and false. Compare weak paraconsistency, the view that true contradictions, and worlds that contain them, are merely a formal tool used to study reasoning.

## o1090 citation: dialetheism_appl

## o1091 entry: strongly connected

strongly connected
A relation R is strongly connected (or total) if and only if, for all x and y, either Rxy, or Ryx.

## o1092 citation

## o1093 entry: structural rule

structural rule
In logic, especially in proof theory, a rule that concerns the manipulation of the components of sequents or deductions without reference to their internal logical structure, such as contraction, weakening, and exchange.

## o1094 entry: subaltern

subaltern
In traditional syllogistic logic, a term describing the relationship between two categorical propositions where the truth of the first (the universal) implies the truth of the second (the particular), but not vice versa.

## o1095 entry: subalternation

subalternation
The logical relationship between a universal statement and its corresponding particular statement, where the truth of the universal necessitates the truth of the particular.

## o1096 entry: subcontraries

subcontraries
In traditional logic, a pair of particular statements (I and O propositions) that cannot both be false together, though both can be true under the square of opposition.

## o1097 entry: subcontrary

subcontrary
Referring to the relationship between two statements that can both be true but cannot both be false at the same time.

## o1098 entry: subformula

subformula
A part of a formula in logic that is itself a formula, often considered in the context of the formula's syntactic structure.

## o1099 entry: subject term

subject term
The term in a categorical proposition that denotes the subject about which something is asserted.

## o1100 entry: subjunctive conditional

subjunctive conditional
A conditional statement expressing what would be the case if its antecedent were true, used to explore hypothetical situations and their consequences.

## o1101 entry: sublanguage

sublanguage
A subset of a language that uses a restricted vocabulary or simpler grammatical structures, often for a specific purpose or domain.

## o1102 entry: sublogic

sublogic
A logical system that is a subset of a more comprehensive logic, retaining some but not all of the operations and principles of the larger system.

## o1103 citation

## o1104 entry: subordinate connective

subordinate connective
A logical connective that occurs within the scope of another logical connective.

## o1105 entry: substitution

substitution
The act of replacing a variable or expression within a logical formula with another, maintaining logical consistency.

## o1106 entry: substitution-instance

substitution-instance
The well-formed formula which results from a given well-formed formula by replacing one or more of the variables occurring in the well-formed formula throughout by some other well-formed formulas, it being understood that each variable so replaced is replaced by the same well-formed formula wherever it occurs.

## o1107 citation: SEP_FreeLogic

## o1108 entry: substitutional quantifier

substitutional quantifier
A type of quantifier interpreted as ranging over expressions or names rather than over objects directly, used in certain theories of reference and meaning.

## o1109 citation

## o1110 entry: substructural logic

substructural logic
A class of non-classical logics that relax or modify structural rules found in classical logic, such as relevance logic and linear logic.

## o1111 entry: subtheory

subtheory
A theory contained within a larger theory, sharing some but not all of its axioms and theorems, and focusing on a subset of its domain.

## o1112 entry: successor function

successor function
A fundamental function in arithmetic and logic that maps any natural number to the next larger natural number, denoted as $S(n) = n+1$.

## o1113 entry: suffix notation

suffix notation
A way of writing expressions where operators follow their operands, also known as reverse Polish notation, used in some calculators and programming languages for its efficiency.

## o1114 entry: supertask

supertask
A task that consists of an infinite sequence of operations completed in a finite amount of time, often discussed in the context of philosophical paradoxes and theoretical physics.

## o1115 entry: supertrue

supertrue
A term used in certain theories of truth, such as supervaluational semantics, to describe propositions that remain true across all precisifications or interpretations of vague terms.

## o1116 citation

## o1117 entry: supervaluational semantics

supervaluational semantics
A semantic theory designed to handle vagueness by considering multiple precisifications of vague terms, with a proposition deemed supertrue if it is true under all precisifications.

## o1118 entry: supposition

supposition
In medieval logic, a relation between an expression and the object or concept that the expression is being used to talk about, where the supposition of the expression need not be its literal reference.

## o1119 entry: suppressed evidence

suppressed evidence
An informal fallacy that occurs when relevant information is omitted from an argument, potentially leading to a misleading or unjustified conclusion.

## o1120 entry: surjection

surjection
A function from one set to another where every element of the target set is mapped to by at least one element of the domain set, also known as an onto function.

## o1121 entry: syllogism

syllogism
A form of deductive reasoning consisting of a major premise, a minor premise, and a conclusion, traditionally used in Aristotelian logic to infer relationships between categories.

## o1122 entry: syllogistic figure

syllogistic figure
The form of a syllogism, determined by the position of the middle term in its premises, categorized into four figures that structure the syllogistic argument differently.

## o1123 citation

## o1124 entry: syllogistic mood

syllogistic mood
