The type of a syllogism, defined by the nature of its premises (universal affirmative, universal negative, particular affirmative, particular negative) and how they combine to form a conclusion.

## o1125 citation

## o1126 entry: syllogistic terms

syllogistic terms
The three terms in a syllogism: the major term (predicate of the conclusion), the minor term (subject of the conclusion), and the middle term (appears in both premises but not in the conclusion). See also barbara.

## o1127 entry: symmetry

symmetry
A property of binary relations where if one element is related to another, then the second is related to the first, such as the relation of equality.

## o1128 citation

## o1129 entry: syncategorematic terms

syncategorematic terms
Terms that do not stand for objects or have a reference by themselves but contribute to the meaning of expressions in which they occur, such as conjunctions, prepositions, and quantifiers.

## o1130 entry: syntactic consequence

syntactic consequence
The relation that holds between premises and the conclusions derivable from them based on axioms and inference rules within a formal system. Contrast semantic consequence.

## o1131 citation

## o1132 entry: syntax

syntax
The set of rules, principles, and processes that govern the structure of sentences in a given language, distinguishing between correct and incorrect forms of expression.

## o1133 section: T

## o1134 entry: Tarskian hierarchy

Tarskian hierarchy
A hierarchical structure of languages proposed by Alfred Tarski to avoid paradoxes in semantic theories, where each level of language can only reference levels below it, preventing self-reference.

## o1135 entry: Tarski's indefinability theorem

Tarski's indefinability theorem
A theorem stating that truth cannot be consistently defined within the same language it applies to, requiring a meta-language for a definition of truth to avoid paradoxes.

## o1136 entry: tautology

tautology
A statement or formula that is true in every possible interpretation, often used in propositional logic to refer to formulas that are logically true.

## o1137 entry: temperature paradox

temperature paradox
A paradox where, from "the temperature is ninety" and "the temperature is rising", it is concluded that "ninety is rising", which seems invalid but can actually be valid under some formalization schemes.

## o1138 entry: temporal modal logic

temporal modal logic
A branch of modal logic that deals with modalities related to time, such as 'always', 'sometimes', and 'never', allowing for reasoning about temporal aspects of propositions.

## o1139 citation

## o1140 entry: term logic

term logic
An approach to logic focusing on the relations between terms in propositions and the inferences that can be drawn from them, characteristic of Aristotelian logic.

## o1141 entry: ternary function

ternary function
A function that takes three arguments or inputs, in contrast to unary and binary functions.

## o1142 entry: ternary relation

ternary relation
A relation involving three elements, where the relation specifies how the elements are connected or related.

## o1143 entry: ternary semantics

ternary semantics
A ternary semantics is a possible worlds semantics that makes use of a ternary relation on possible worlds instead of, or in addition to, the more standard binary accessibility relation. Ternary semantics have been most widely applied in the development of relevant logics.

## o1144 citation

## o1145 entry: theorem

theorem
A statement or proposition that has been formally proven on the basis of previously established statements or axioms within a logical or mathematical system.

## o1146 entry: theory

theory
A coherent set of propositions or statements, especially one that forms a comprehensive explanation of some aspect of the natural world or an abstract concept.

## o1147 entry: three-valued logic

three-valued logic
A logical system that introduces a third truth value (such as 'unknown', 'indeterminate', or 'both true and false') in addition to the classic binary true and false values.

## o1148 entry: tilde

tilde
The symbol '~' used in logic to denote negation, indicating that the proposition it precedes is false.

## o1149 entry: token

token
An instance of a type, such as a particular occurrence of a word or phrase, in contrast to the abstract concept or category it represents.

## o1150 entry: tolerant

tolerant
In the theory of vagueness, a predicate is considered tolerant if, and only if, small changes in the relevant underlying properties of an object do not affect the justice with which the predicate applies to it. Thus, the predicate "bald" is tolerant, since one hair more or less does not transform a clear instance of baldness into a clear instance of non-baldness.

## o1151 entry: tonk

tonk
A fictional logical connective introduced to illustrate the importance of preserving inference rules in defining logical operators, showing that arbitrary rules can lead to absurdity.

## o1152 citation

## o1153 citation

## o1154 entry: top

top
In logic, a symbol (⊤) representing the highest or maximal element in a lattice or order, often used to denote a tautology or universally true proposition in propositional logic.

## o1155 entry: topos

topos
See elementary topos

## o1156 entry: topos theory

topos theory
The study of toposes, which are categories that behave like the category of sets and provide a foundation for much of mathematics, allowing for generalized notions of computation and logic.

## o1157 entry: total function

total function
A function that is defined for every element of its domain, ensuring that there is an output for each input.

## o1158 entry: transition function

transition function
See action table.

## o1159 entry: transitive closure

transitive closure
The smallest transitive relation that contains a given relation, effectively adding the minimum necessary elements to make the original relation transitive.

## o1160 entry: transitivity

transitivity
A property of a relation where if the relation holds between A and B, and between B and C, then it also holds between A and C, ensuring a kind of consistency or continuity in the relation across elements.

## o1161 entry: translation

translation
A translation is a function from the expressions of one language to the expressions of another language. Translations are typically intended to preserve either the meanings or the truth conditions of the translated expressions.

## o1162 entry: translation lemma

translation lemma
Synonym for the S theorem.

## o1163 entry: transposition

transposition
A logical rule that allows the equivalent transformation of a conditional statement $P \rightarrow Q$ into its contrapositive $\neg Q \rightarrow \neg P$, preserving truth.

## o1164 entry: trans-world identity

trans-world identity
A concept in modal logic and metaphysics concerning the identity of individuals across different possible worlds, addressing questions of persistence and change.

## o1165 entry: tribar

tribar
A name for the symbol ≡, used for logical equivalence.

## o1166 citation

## o1167 entry: trichotomy

trichotomy
A relation R is trichotomous (or comparable) if and only if, for any objects x and y, either Rxy, or Ryx, or x = y.

## o1168 citation

## o1169 entry: trichotomy law

trichotomy law
A law in order theory and mathematics stating that for any two elements in a certain set, exactly one of three relationships (greater than, less than, or equal to) must hold.

## o1170 entry: triviality

triviality
The state or quality of being trivial, in logic and mathematics, often referring to statements, propositions, or problems that are oversimplified or of little interest or importance.

## o1171 entry: truth

truth
A concept in logic and philosophy concerning the property of statements, beliefs, or propositions corresponding to reality or fact, or being in accord with the actual state of affairs.

## o1172 entry: truth conditions

truth conditions
The conditions under which a statement or proposition is considered to be true, playing a central role in the semantic analysis of language.

## o1173 entry: truth function

truth function
A function that takes truth values as input and produces a truth value as output, used in logic to model the truth conditions of logical connectives.

## o1174 entry: truth-functional

truth-functional
Pertaining to an operator or connective in logic whose output truth value depends solely on the input truth values, without regard to the content of the propositions involved.

## o1175 citation

## o1176 entry: truth-in-a-model

truth-in-a-model
A concept in semantic theories of truth indicating that a statement or proposition is true relative to a particular model or interpretation of the language in which it is expressed.

## o1177 citation

## o1178 entry: truthmaker

truthmaker
An entity or set of entities in the world that makes a proposition true, grounding truth in aspects of reality.

## o1179 entry: truth predicate

truth predicate
A predicate that assigns the property of being true to propositions, often discussed in relation to Tarski's semantic conception of truth and the liar paradox.

## o1180 entry: truth table

truth table
A table used in logic to show the truth value of a compound statement for every possible combination of truth values of its components, instrumental in analyzing logical expressions.

## o1181 entry: truth-teller

truth-teller
The converse of the liar paradox, a statement that asserts its own truth, raising questions about self-reference and the nature of truth.

## o1182 citation

## o1183 entry: truth-tree

truth-tree
Synonym of analytic tableaux.

## o1184 entry: truth value

truth value
The value indicating the truth or falsity of a proposition or statement, typically represented as true or false in classical logic, but possibly more varied in many-valued logics.

## o1185 entry: truth-value gap

truth-value gap
A situation where a statement or proposition cannot be assigned a traditional truth value of true or false, often due to vagueness or undefined terms.

## o1186 citation: Logic Without Gaps or Gluts

## o1187 entry: truth-value glut

truth-value glut
A condition in which a statement or proposition is paradoxically both true and false simultaneously, associated with dialetheism and contradictions.

## o1188 citation: Logic Without Gaps or Gluts

## o1189 entry: T-schema

T-schema
The Tarski schema for defining truth, stating that 'P' is true if and only if P, where 'P' is a placeholder for a proposition and P is the proposition itself.

## o1190 entry: tu quoque

tu quoque
A logical fallacy that attempts to discredit an opponent's position by asserting the opponent's failure to act consistently with that position, essentially accusing them of hypocrisy.

## o1191 entry: Turing computable function

Turing computable function
A function that can be calculated by a Turing machine, representing the class of functions that are computable in principle, according to the Church–Turing thesis.

## o1192 entry: Turing thesis

Turing thesis
See Church–Turing thesis.

## o1193 entry: turnstile

turnstile
A symbol used in logic ($\vdash$) to denote syntactic entailment or provability, indicating that the statement or set of statements to the right is a logical consequence of the statements to the left within a given formal system.

## o1194 entry: type

type
(In type theory.) A category or class of entities that share certain characteristics, used in logic and mathematics to distinguish between different kinds of objects, expressions, or variables, preventing certain kinds of logical paradoxes.
(In the type-token distinction.) A universal, as opposed to a particular..

## o1195 entry: type theory

type theory
A framework in mathematical logic and computer science that uses types to classify expressions and objects, aiming to avoid paradoxes like Russell's paradox by organizing objects into hierarchies or levels and restricting operations to objects of the same type.

## o1196 section: U

## o1197 entry: unary function

unary function
A function that operates on a single input or argument, common in mathematics and logic for representing operations like negation or the absolute value function.

## o1198 entry: unary relation

unary relation
A relation that applies to a single element, used to characterize properties or attributes that an element may or may not possess.

## o1199 entry: universal elimination

universal elimination
A rule of inference in predicate logic that allows for the derivation of a specific statement about an individual from a general statement that applies to all members of a category.

## o1200 entry: universal introduction

universal introduction
A rule of inference in predicate logic that allows for the generalization of a statement to all members of a category if the statement is shown to hold for an arbitrary but specific individual.

## o1201 entry: universal proposition

universal proposition
A statement in logic that asserts something about all members of a certain category, typically formulated using a universal quantifier.

## o1202 entry: universal quantifier

universal quantifier
A symbol in predicate logic ($\forall$) used to indicate that the statement it precedes holds for all individuals in the domain of discourse.

## o1203 entry: universal variable

universal variable
A variable in predicate logic that is bound by a universal quantifier, representing any and all individuals in the domain of discourse.

## o1204 entry: universe of discourse

universe of discourse
The set of all objects, individuals, or values that are relevant in a particular logical or mathematical discussion, serving as the domain over which quantifiers range.

## o1205 entry: upward Löwenheim–Skolem theorem

upward Löwenheim–Skolem theorem
Part of the Löwenheim–Skolem theorem.

## o1206 entry: unsound deductive argument

unsound deductive argument
An argument that is either invalid in its logical form or contains at least one false premise, and therefore does not guarantee the truth of its conclusion.

## o1207 entry: use

use
In philosophy, particularly in the analysis of language, "use" refers to the actual application of a word or a phrase in a sentence to convey meaning. In the use-mention distinction, "use" involves employing words to refer to things, actions, qualities, or concepts in the world. For example, in the sentence "I enjoy reading books," the word "books" is used to refer to objects that can be read; it is not merely mention.

## o1208 section: V

## o1209 entry: vacuous quantifier

vacuous quantifier
A vacuous quantifier is one that doesn't bind any variables, such as the second quantifier in $\forall x \forall y Rxx$.

## o1210 citation

## o1211 entry: vagueness

vagueness
The characteristic of terms, concepts, or propositions that lack clear boundaries or precision in meaning, leading to indeterminate or borderline cases.

## o1212 entry: valid

valid
A deductive argument whose structure ensures that if all the premises are true, then the conclusion must also be true, demonstrating logical validity.
In model-theoretic semantics, a formula that is true under all interpretations.
In proof-theoretic semantics, a formula that is either an explicit rule of inference of a system, or that does not allow one to prove anything that could not be proved using the explicit rules of inference.

## o1213 citation

## o1214 entry: valuation

valuation
Another name for an interpretation of a formal language, coming from the fact that it assigns semantic values (such as truth values) to the language's formulas.

## o1215 citation

## o1216 entry: variable assignment

variable assignment
Sometimes, an interpretation of a formal language for first-order or higher-order logic is called simply a "variable assignment", or "variable assignment function".

## o1217 citation

## o1218 citation

## o1219 entry: vel

vel
The Latin word for "or", used in logic as a name for ∨, the descending wedge symbol. The symbol is used to denote a disjunction that is inclusive, meaning at least one of the disjuncts must be true for the whole expression to be true.

## o1220 entry: verification constraint

verification constraint
See epistemic constraint.

## o1221 entry: verity

verity
The verity (or "degree of truth") of a statement is the semantic value of that statement within degree-theoretic semantics, which assigns degrees between 0 and 1 to statements.

## o1222 citation

## o1223 entry: verum

verum
Verum (Latin for "true") is another name for the $\top$ symbol, which represents a primitive, necessarily true statement, and is sometimes considered a nullary connective.

## o1224 citation

## o1225 entry: vicious circle principle

vicious circle principle
A principle against definitions or arguments that are circular, ensuring that the thing being defined is not used in its own definition or premise in a way that presupposes its conclusion.

## o1226 section: W

## o1227 entry: weak completeness

weak completeness
The property of a logical system where if a statement is semantically valid (true under all interpretations), then there is a proof of the statement within the system.

## o1228 citation

## o1229 entry: weak counterexample

weak counterexample
Within intuitionistic logic and intuitionistic mathematics, a weak counterexample is a situation in which we have no positive evidence for the (intuitionistic) truth of some instance of the law of excluded middle, $p \lor \neg p$.

## o1230 citation

## o1231 citation

## o1232 entry: weak excluded middle

weak excluded middle
A principle in intuitionistic logic stating that for any proposition P, either P is provable or not-P is provable, but not necessarily both, reflecting a more nuanced view of truth than the classical law of excluded middle.

## o1233 entry: weak Kleene connectives

weak Kleene connectives
Connectives defined in the three-valued logic of Kleene, which do not fully determine the truth value of compound statements if any component statement is undefined.

## o1234 entry: weak mathematical induction

weak mathematical induction
A form of mathematical induction that only assumes the truth of the statement for the immediately preceding case to prove its truth for any natural number, as opposed to strong induction, which assumes the statement for all smaller numbers.

## o1235 entry: weak negation

weak negation
A form of negation in some non-classical logics where the negation of a proposition does not assert the truth of the opposite proposition but rather the absence of truth of the original proposition.

## o1236 entry: weak paraconsistency

weak paraconsistency
Weak paraconsistency is the view that true contradictions, and worlds that contain them, are merely a formal tool used to study reasoning. Compare strong paraconsistency, the view that there are possible worlds where contradictions are true, or where some statements are both true and false.

## o1237 entry: weakening

weakening
A rule in both propositional and predicate logic allowing the addition of propositions to a derivation without affecting its validity, reflecting the idea that if something follows from a set of premises, it also follows from any larger set of premises.

## o1238 entry: wedge

wedge
A symbol used in logic ($\wedge$) to represent logical conjunction, indicating that both connected statements are true.

## o1239 entry: well-formed formula

well-formed formula
A string of symbols in a formal language that follows the syntactic rules of the language, making it a meaningful or grammatically correct expression within the context of that system.

## o1240 entry: wff

wff
Short for well-formed formula. Pronounced "woof", or sometimes "wiff", "weff", or "whiff". (All sources supported "woof". The sources cited for "wiff", "weff", and "whiff" gave these pronunciations as alternatives to "woof". Gensler gives "wood" and "woofer" as examples of how to pronounce the vowel in "woof".) Plural "wffs".

## o1241 citation: GenslerLogic

## o1242 citation

## o1243 citation

## o1244 citation

## o1245 citation

## o1246 citation

## o1247 citation

## o1248 citation

## o1249 citation

## o1250 citation

## o1251 section: Y

## o1252 entry: Yablo paradox

Yablo paradox
A paradox involving an infinite sequence of sentences, each of which states that all following sentences in the sequence are false. Unlike the liar paradox, it does not rely on self-reference, raising questions about the nature of paradoxes and infinity.

## o1253 section: Z

## o1254 entry: Zeno paradoxes

Zeno paradoxes
A series of paradoxes proposed by the ancient Greek philosopher Zeno of Elea to challenge the coherence of the concepts of plurality, motion, and the continuum, including the famous paradoxes of Achilles and the tortoise, and the dichotomy.
